Moduli spaces of quadratic differentials: Abel-Jacobi map and deformation
Yu Qiu

Abstract
. We study the moduli space of framed quadratic differentials with prescribed singularities parameterized by a decorated marked surface with punctures (DMSp), where simple zeros, double poles and higher order poles respectively correspond to decorations, punctures and boundary components. We show that the fundamental group of this space equals the kernel of the Abel-Jacobi (AJ) map from the surface mapping class group of DMSp to the first homology group of the marked surface (without decorations/punctures). Moreover, a universal cover of this space is given by the space of stability conditions on the associated 3-Calabi-Yau category. Furthermore, when we partially compactify and orbifold this moduli space by allowing the collision of simple zeros and some of the double poles, the resulting moduli space is isomorphic to a quotient of the space of stability conditions on the deformed (with respect to those collidable double poles) 3-Calabi-Yau category. Finally, we show that the fundamental group of this partially compactified orbifold equals the quotient group of the kernel of the AJ map by the square of any point-pushing diffeomorphism around any collidable double pole. This construction can produces any non-exceptional spherical/Euclidean Artin braid groups. Key words: quadratic differentials, Abel-Jacobi maps, partial compactification, stability conditions, flip graphs, Artin braid groups



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Topology of moduli spaces of quadratic differentials

The moduli spaces of abelian/quadratic differentials are classical objects of study
in dynamical systems, which also naturally interact with topology, algebraic geometry
and, recently, representation theory of algebras.
For instance, the topology of moduli spaces has attracted a lot of attention.

Connected components

Kontsevich-Zorich
[
KZ2
]
classified the connected components of the space of holomorphic abelian differentials;
later Lanneau
[
Lan
]
and Boissy
[
Boi
]
treated the case of meromorphic quadratic differentials;
more recently, Chen-Gendron
[
CG
]
extended this the case of
k
-differentials.
In most of these cases, the components are classified by spin invariants, hyper-ellipticity and torsion conditions.

Fundamental groups

The next interesting and challenging question is the calculation of (orbifold) fundamental groups of components of these spaces.
Until now, few cases have been known: see
[
Wal
,
Ham
,
CS2
]
for the studies on certain quotient groups of the fundamental groups of such types,
[
FM
]
and
[
LM
]
for the genus 2 and 3 cases respectively.

In the previous work with King
[
KQ2
]
,
we calculated the connected components and fundamental groups of moduli spaces of quadratic differentials
with only simple zeros and higher order poles,
in which case, the prescribed singularities are encoded by a decorated marked surface (DMS)
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
.
One of the key ingredients is the introduction of the braid twist group
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta})\lhd\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta})
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
in
[
Qy2
]
, generated by the braid twists along any closed arcs).
Such a group is a (usually proper) normal subgroup of the surface braid group
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta})
.
The motivation came from the study of the spherical twist group of the Calabi-Yau-3 category associated to
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
, where these two (braid and spherical) twist groups can be naturally identified (
[
Qy2
, Thm. 1]
).

In this work, we investigate the case with simple zeros, higher order poles and also double poles.
The first key observation is that the braid twist group is in fact,
the kernel of the Abel-Jacobi map.
In the finite area case (no double or higher order poles), this map was considered in
[
Wal
]
.

Topological Abel-Jacobi maps

We fix a Riemann surface
\mathbf{X}
and consider the quadratic differential
\phi
of
prescribed singularity type

(\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}=\mathbf{w}_{\geq-1},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}=\mathbf{w}_{\leq-2}),

where
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}
and
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}
are the collections of the finite/infinite singularities, respectively,
represented by their orders.
The numerical datum is encoded by a weighted DMS with punctures,

\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
1
1

1

The symbol
☉
is pronounced
sun
.

which is the real blow-up of
\mathbf{X}
with respect to
\phi
(as a topological surface).
The weighted decorations in
\Delta
are finite singularities (=zeros and simple poles) in
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}
;
the punctures in
☉
are order two poles in
\mathbf{w}_{-2}
;
the marked points
\mathbf{M}\subset\partial\mathbf{S}
on the boundary components correspond to
(the distinguish directions at) higher order poles in
\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}
.
We will mainly focus on the moduli space
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed quadratic differentials.
It is a manifold and a
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
-covering of the (unframed) moduli space
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

The monodromy map naturally maps each element in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
to
a configuration
\Delta
of weighted points on
\mathbf{X}
with special marking
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}
.
The classical Abel-Jacobi map
\operatorname{AJ}^{\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}}
maps
special marking
\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}(\subset\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})
, regarded as a divisor of
\mathbf{X}
,
to a line bundle in the Picard variety
\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3})
.
Note that we forget about the punctures in
\text{\Sun}=\mathbf{w}_{-2}
here.
Then we obtain the following morphisms:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{conf}_{\Delta}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}^{\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}}}\operatorname{Pic}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}).

Taking
\pi_{1}
of these spaces, we obtain a sequence of maps:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\Xi}\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\Delta})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}),

where the second term is the surface braid group and the last term is the first homology.
We call the map
\operatorname{AJ}
induced from
\operatorname{AJ}^{\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}}
the
(topological) Abel-Jacobi map
.

We will focus on the simple zero case, i.e.
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}=\mathbf{w}_{1}
.
We will also consider the Teichmüller framed (i.e.
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed) version
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
which is the
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
-covering of
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
The following is the main result of the paper (
Theorem

6.9
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
be a DMS with punctures. There is a short exact sequence

(1.3)

\displaystyle 1\to\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\Xi}\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})\to 1

Moreover, we have

(1.4)

\begin{cases}\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=1,\\
\pi_{0}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}),\end{cases}

where
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is any connected component of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

The short exact sequence (
1.3
) is equivalent to (
Corollary

6.11
):

(1.5)

\displaystyle 1\to\pi_{1}\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\Xi}\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\widehat{\operatorname{AJ}}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})\to 1,

where
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
is the unframed orbifold, which will appear in the later discussion.

When
\text{\Sun}=\varnothing
, the theorem is a slight improvement of the main result in
[
KQ2
]
.

In the works
[
Wal
,
Ham
,
CS2
]
, the authors studied maps similar to (
1.2
)
and described the image of the corresponding
\Xi
.
In our case, the most challenging part is the injectivity of
\Xi
.
Notice that in general, injectivity does not holds,
cf.
[
Ham
, Rem. 12.4]
and
[
Gia
, Thm. A]
.

K(\pi,1)
-conjecture and categorification

In
[
KZ1
]
,
Kontsevich-Zorich conjectured that ‘each connected component of (a stratum of the) moduli space of quadratic differentials has homotopy type
K(\pi,1)
for
\pi
which is a group commensurable with some mapping class group’.
At least in our case (i.e. all finite singularities are simple zeros), we believe that is true.
In fact, it is closely related to the contractibility conjecture for the spaces of stability conditions.

The notion of stability condition on triangulated categories was introduced by Bridgeland
[
B1
]
,
with motivations coming from
\Pi
-stability in string theory.
By the work of
[
HKK
,
CHQ
]
,
any connected component of a moduli space of quadratic differentials with prescribed singularity type
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}}

can be realized by the space of stability conditions on some triangulated category
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}
:

\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}})\cong\operatorname{Stab}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}})

Such a category
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}
is not unique and can be chosen to be Calabi-Yau.
One should regard that
\operatorname{Stab}\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}
provides a categorification of the moduli space;
other topological operations (e.g. collision and collapsing subsurface)
may also be understood by some categorical operations (e.g. taking Verdier quotient in
[
BMQS
]
).
In particular, we will use the 3-Calabi-Yau categories
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
constructed in
[
CHQ
]
,
to prove the main result above.
More precisely, we show the following (
Theorem

6.8
).

Theorem 1.2
.

\operatorname{Stab}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))
is simply connected and hence is a universal cover of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Donaldson-Thomas (DT) invariants and quantum dilogarithm identities

Motivated by classical curve-counting problems,
Thomas
[
Tho
]
proposed the so-called DT-invariants to count stable sheaves on Calabi–Yau 3-folds.
Kontsevich–Soibelman
[
KS1
,
KS2
]
generalized their work and formalized a way of counting semistable objects
in 3-Calabi–Yau triangulated (
A_{\infty}
-)categories (or counting BPS states in physics terminology),
using the so-called motivic DT-invariants.
The quantum version of DT-invariants can be calculated via a product of quantum dilogarithms cf.
[
Ke2
,
Rei
,
KS1
,
KS2
,
KW
]
.
Recently Haiden
[
Hai
]
and Kidwai-Williams
[
KW
]
gave formulae for calculating the quantum DT-invariants in the surface case by counting saddle trajectories and ring domains

The point is that one can think of the DT-invariants of abelian categories
(or hearts of some triangulated category) as a function on the spaces of stability conditions.
It is constant in a fixed cell and changes values when crossing a wall (of the first kind).
The fact that the product is an invariant is due to quantum dilogarithm identities (QDI).
In many cases, the QDI reduce to the pentagon identity (and the trivial commutation relation or square identity),
(e.g.
[
Qy1
]
for the Dynkin case, cf.
[
Na1
,
Na2
]
).
In the marked surface case, such a reducibility (to square/pentagon identity) is equivalent to
the fact that the fundamental group of the unoriented flip exchange graph
\underline{\operatorname{EG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
of triangulations
is generated by squares and pentagons,
cf.
Theorem

3.1
for the classical result,
[
FST
, Thm. 10.2]
for the cluster case and
our slight generalization
Proposition

B.2
.
Such results interpreted as local simply connectedness.

1.2.
Techniques

Mixed twist group

(
Section

2
)

The surface braid group
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})

was first introduced by Zariski (naturally generalizing Artin’s geometric definition)
and was rediscovered by Fox (cf. the survey
[
GJP
, § 2]
and the references).
There are two types of standard generators:
the braid twist and the L-twists (point-pushing diffeomorphisms)
see
Figure

2
or
Figure

1
for the monodromy version in the moduli spaces.

Figure 1.
Loops in moduli spaces as braid twist/L-twist

The first step of proving
Theorem

1.1
is to understand the kernel of the Abel-Jacobi map in (
1.3
).
For this purpose, we introduce the
mixed twist groups

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})
,
which is the subgroup of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
generated by all braid twists and
the L-twists that wind a puncture in a chosen subset
2
2

2

The symbol
\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}
are pronounced
left/right moon
respectively.

\leftmoon
of
☉
. Denote by
{\rightmoon}=\text{\Sun}\smallsetminus\leftmoon
.

We prove that (
Theorem

2.6
)
the mixed twist group
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})
is the kernel of the (relative) Abel-Jacobi map
\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}
:

(1.6)

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})=\ker\big(\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})\big),

which admits a finite presentation in
Theorem

2.12
.

Here,
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})=\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})\oplus\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})
for
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})=\mathbb{Z}^{\oplus{\rightmoon}}
.
In particular, the biggest one is

(1.7)

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\text{\Sun}})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

for
\operatorname{AJ}=\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\text{\Sun}}
that appears in (
1.4
),
and the smallest mixed twist group is the braid twist group

\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\varnothing})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\varnothing}
.
The inbetweeners will appear when we calculating the fundamental groups of partial compactifications of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
later.

Flip groupoids and flip twist groups

(
Sections

3
,
4
and
5
)

The other ingredient of proving
Theorem

1.1
is the study of fundamental groups of oriented flip graphs.
As mentioned in the DT-invariant paragraph, QDI is equivalent to the fact that

\pi_{1}\underline{\operatorname{EG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
is generated by unoriented squares and pentagons.
We need to analyze the fundamental group
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
of the flip graph of decorated triangulations of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
There are four types of basic loops:

•

The squares/pentagons lifted from
\pi_{1}\underline{\operatorname{EG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, see
Figure

12
.

•

The short dumbbell relations introduced in
[
KQ2
]
, see (
4.3
) and cf.
Figure

12
.

•

The long dumbbell relations, see
Figure

14
and
Figure

20
.

•

The symmetric hexagons, see
Figure

14
and
Figure

21
.

When imposing these relations/faces in the oriented version
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
of the flip graph for
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
, e.g. the purple graph in the right picture of
Figure

11
,
we obtain the
flip groupoid

\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, as a generalization of cluster exchange groupoid introduced in
[
KQ2
]
.
The subgroup of the point group at a triangulation
\mathrm{T}
of
\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
generated by local flip twists, is the
flip twist group

\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
, cf.
Definition

4.3
.

We show that there is a natural isomorphism between
a flip twist group and the corresponding mixed twist group (
Theorem

5.8
):

(1.8)

\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon(\mathbb{T})}),

where
\mathrm{T}
is the triangulation of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
induced from a triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
, forgetting about the decorations, and
\leftmoon(\mathbb{T})
is the set of
\mathbb{T}
-isolated punctures (which are in self-folded
\mathbb{T}
-triangles).

A highlight of the proof is that the higher braid relation

\eta\delta\eta\delta=\delta\eta\delta\eta
decomposes into 2 long dumbbell hexagons and 2 symmetric hexagons
as shown in
Figure

15
, which is very different from
the other decomposition (via folding in cluster theory, cf.
[
HHQ
]
)
into non-symmetric hexagons shown in
Figure

17
.

1.3.
Partial compactification with orbifolding via deformation

After we study the mixed/flip twist groups, there is a strong hint
of studying the various compactifications of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
in particular their categorifications using spaces of stability conditions.
Recently, there are many works on the topic of compactifying spaces of stability conditions,
including:
[
BDL
,
KKO
]
of Thurston type compactification;

[
Bol
,
BPPW
]
of partial compactification
and
[
BMQS
,
BMS
]
towards multi-scale compactification in the sense of
[
BCGGM
]
.
In
Appendix

B
, we give a result of a similar nature.

The other natural moduli space

The moduli space
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
and 3-Calabi-Yau category
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
in
Theorem

1.2
are
the natural ones associated to
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
from the point of view of dynamical systems and of higher categories (i.e. perverse schobers, cf.
[
CHQ
]
).
Also, recent work
[
KW
]
suggests the same from the point of view of DT-theory (and mathematical physics),

However, they are
unnatural
from the point of view of cluster theory and representation theory of algebras (cf.
[
FST
,
BS
]
), at least for the first glance.
Indeed, associated to a triangulation
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
,
the quiver with potential
Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
with 3-cycles potential
W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
for
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is
degenerate
,
in the sense of
[
DWZ
]
.
On the other hand, the unique
non-degenerate
quiver with potential

(Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\YinYang}},W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\YinYang}})
3
3

3

The symbol
☯
is pronounced
Yin-Yang
,
meaning
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry in some sense.

is the cluster favorite (cf.
[
L-F
]
).
Indeed, the mutations of which match the original quiver mutations in the sense of
[
FZ
]
and
the Jacobian algebras are of skew-gentle type, generalizing type
D_{n}
and
\widetilde{D_{n}}
.
Fomin-Shapiro-Thurston
[
FST
]
introduced the extra
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry according to cluster theory,
for each puncture (which we will call it a vortex).
The corresponding interpretation in the moduli spaces of quadratic differentials
is the inclusion of signs of residue at double poles and
allowing the collision of simple zeros and double poles, cf.
[
GMN
,
BS
]
.

The comparison

(App.
B
)

In the main theorem

(1.9)

\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})=\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})/\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})\cong\operatorname{Stab}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}}))/\operatorname{Aut}^{\circ}

of
[
BS
, Thm. 1.2]
(comparing to the quotient version (
6.13
) of
Theorem

6.6
),

•

The moduli space
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})
is a partial compactification (with orbifolding) of
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

•

The 3-Calabi-Yau category
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})
is a deformation of
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
constructed from
(Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\YinYang}},W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\YinYang}})
that provides categorification of the corresponding cluster algebra).

We show that the deformation process of the 3-Calabi-Yau categories in
Theorem

1.2

perfectly matches with the partial compactification (with orbifolding) process.

More precisely, we can choose a partition
\text{\Sun}=\leftmoon\cup{\rightmoon}
,
call punctures in
{\rightmoon}
by vortices and use
☯

for
{\rightmoon}
to indicate the extra
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry associated.
Roughly speaking,
by subtracting the cycles around puncture in
☯
from the potential
W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}

we obtain a deformation
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Correspondingly, we first allow the collision of simple zeros with double poles in
{\rightmoon}

and get a partial compactification
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.
Then by orbifolding the extra strata we get an orbifold
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
.
Note that a tricky thing is that we are actually
partially compactify and orifold
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
instead of the unframed
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

since we need to distinguish usual punctures and the collidable one.

We show the following (
Theorem

B.15
) for the unframed version of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
.

Theorem 1.3
.

There is an isomorphism

\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\colon=\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})/\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})\cong\operatorname{Stab}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}))/\operatorname{Aut}^{\circ}

between complex orbifolds,
which specializes to (
6.13
) in
[
CHQ
]
for
(\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang})=(\text{\Sun},\varnothing)

and (
1.9
) in
[
BS
]
for
(\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang})=(\varnothing,\text{\Sun})
.

In
[
BMQS
]
, collapsing surfaces include the special case of collision of singularities.
However, one uses quotient categories for the extra (lower) strata there
while we use deformed categories for the unions with the extra strata.

Fundamental groups of compactified spaces with/without orbifolding

(§
7
)

We end the paper with the calculation of the fundamental groups of
the partial compactifications of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
namely,
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
(without orbifolding) and
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
(with orbifolding),
cf.
Theorem

7.4
and
Theorem

7.7
.

Theorem 1.4
.

The fundamental groups of compactified moduli spaces are

(1.10)

\begin{cases}\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\mu({\rightmoon})\\
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\mu^{2}({\rightmoon}).\end{cases}

where
\mu({\rightmoon})
consisting of loops of the form in
Figure

19
, where the puncture is in
{\rightmoon}

and
\mu^{2}({\rightmoon})
consisting of the square of the loops above.

Denote by
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
the DMS with mixed punctures & vortices (=DMSx).
The difference from
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
to
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
is that when considering associated symmetric groups,
one quotients by the (normal) subgroup
\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})
,
generated by squares of L-twist along any L-arc enclosing precisely one puncture in
{\rightmoon}
.
Then we have DMSx version of (
1.3
),which is equivalent to the second equation in (
1.10
):

(1.11)

\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})=\ker\big(\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})\big).

A key ingredient of the seminal works
[
FST
,
BS
]
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}
-symmetry mentioned above.
Our topological interpretation of which is

\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\text{\YinYang})\colon=\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})\big/\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}(\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon}))\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}.

Hence such a symmetry is hidden naturally in (
1.11
).

Euclidean Artin braid groups and hyperplane arrangements

(App.
C
)

We have a byproduct by examples:
for any non-exceptional Artin braid group
\operatorname{Br}(\text{\Aries})
of (spherical or) Euclidean type
♈
,
there is a DMSx
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
such that

\operatorname{Br}(\text{\Aries})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})=\ker\big(\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\YinYang}}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})\big).

The mixed twist group
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})
here is the quotient of
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
in (
1.11
) by
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}
.

Moreover, the corresponding hyperplane arrangements
\mathfrak{h}^{\operatorname{reg}}_{\text{\Aries}}/W_{\text{\Aries}}
, quotient by the Coxeter/Weyl group
W_{\text{\Aries}}
,
can be realized by

\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})=\operatorname{Stab}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}))/\operatorname{Br}(\text{\Aries}),

the moduli space of signed quadratic differentials on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
or quotient of stability space on the associated 3-Calabi-Yau category (cf.
Theorem

C.4
).
This provides another justification for orbifolding in partial compactification.
Such a result is also the 3-Calabi-Yau analogue of
[
B2
]
for Kleinian singularities.

Convention

•

Composition:
we will write
a\circ b
as
ab
.

•

Inverse
we will write
s^{-1}
as
\underline{s}
and
\underline{st}=\underline{s}\cdot\underline{t}
.

•

Conjugation:
we will write
\underline{b}ab
as
a^{b}
and then
\underline{(a^{b})}=\underline{a}^{b}
.

•

Relations:
we will use the higher braid relation of rank
m
:

\operatorname{Br}^{m}(a,b)\colon\underbrace{aba\cdots}_{m}=\underbrace{bab\cdots}_{m}.

In particular,
we will often use commutation relation
\operatorname{Co}=\operatorname{Br}^{2}
and usual braid relation
\operatorname{Br}=\operatorname{Br}^{3}
.
And the only other higher rank braid relation we will consider in this paper is
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
.

•

There is a partition
\text{\Sun}=\leftmoon\cup{\rightmoon}
and

☯
is the same set of
{\rightmoon}
but equipped with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-symmetry.

2.
Topological Abel-Jacobi maps and mixed twist groups

2.1.
Decorated marked surface with punctures (DMSp)

Let
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}=(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M},\text{\Sun},\Delta)
be a
decorated marked surface with punctures
,
which is determined by the following data:

•

\mathbf{S}
is a genus
g
surface;

•

A set
\mathbf{M}\neq\varnothing
of
marked points
in the boundary
\partial\mathbf{S}
of
\mathbf{S}
;

•

A set
☉
of
punctures
in the interior
\mathbf{S}^{\circ}
of
\mathbf{S}
;

•

A set
\Delta
of
decorations
in
\mathbf{S}^{\circ}
.

We require that each boundary component of
\partial\mathbf{S}
contains at least one marked point.
Let
m=|\mathbf{M}|
and

\text{\Sun}=\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{p}\},\quad\Delta=\{Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{w}\}

for
p=|\text{\Sun}|
,
w=|\Delta|
.
In the figures, we usual
draw marked points/punctures/decorations as black bullets, suns and red circles
(i.e.
\bullet
/
☉
/
\circ
), respectively.

We will denote by
\mathbf{S}=(\mathbf{S},\mathbf{M})
the marked surface (without punctures and decorations),

\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
the decorated marked surface (DMS, forgetting about the punctures) and

\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
the marked surface with punctures (forgetting about the decorations).

Denote by
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
the
(mapping class) group
of isotopy classes of homeomorphisms of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}

that preserves
\mathbf{M}
,
☉
and
\Delta
setwise, respectively.
Similarly for other variations of
\operatorname{MCG}
when forgetting about certain sets of points.

The DMSp
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
can be regarded as a point in the (unordered) configuration space
\operatorname{conf}_{\Delta}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

of
w=|\Delta|
points in
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
The
surface braid group
is defined to be

\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\colon=\pi_{1}\operatorname{conf}_{\Delta}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}).

Alternatively, consider the forgetful map
F^{\Delta}\colon\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
with induced map

(2.1)

F^{\Delta}_{*}\colon\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}).

Then it is well-known that
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\ker F_{*}^{\Delta}

or there is a short exact sequence

(2.2)

\displaystyle 1\to\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{F^{\Delta}_{*}}\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\to 1.

For the later use, we introduce the following notions.

•

An
open arc
is a curve connecting marked points or punctures,
which will be usual drawn in blue/green colorway.

•

A
closed arc
is a curve connecting decorations,
which will be usual drawn in red/orange colorway.

We say a closed arc is
simple
if it has no self-intersection and its endpoints are different
and an L-arc is a closed arc with endpoints coincide and without self-intersection in
\mathbf{S}^{\circ}
.
Denote by
\operatorname{CA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
and
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
the sets of simple closed arcs and of L-arcs in
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
, respectively.

Along any arc
\eta
in
\operatorname{CA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
, there is a
braid twist

B_{\eta}
in
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
which only moves the two endpoints/decorations of
\eta
anticlockwise within a neighbourhood of
\eta
,
as shown in the upper pictures of
Figure

2
.
Along any arc
\zeta
in
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
, there is an
L-twist (or point-pushing diffeomorphisms)

L_{\zeta}
in
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
which corresponds to drag the endpoint/decoration of
\zeta
along
\zeta
anticlockwise in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{conf}_{\Delta}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, as shown in the lower pictures of
Figure

2
.

η
\bullet
\circ
\bullet
\circ
\operatorname{B}_{\eta}
η

\circ
\bullet
\circ
\bullet
\circ

\bullet
\circ

Figure 2.
The braid twist and L-twist

These are the two standard generators of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
(cf.
Theorem

2.4
),
which satisfies the following conjugation formula:

(2.3)

\begin{cases}B_{\Psi(\eta)}=\Psi\circ B_{\eta}\circ\Psi^{-1}=B_{\eta}^{\Psi^{-1}},\\
L_{\Psi(\zeta)}=\Psi\circ L_{\zeta}\circ\Psi^{-1}=L_{\zeta}^{\Psi^{-1}},\\
\end{cases}

for any
\Psi\in\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
\eta
in
\operatorname{CA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
and
\zeta\in\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
We will brief
B_{\eta}
and
L_{\zeta}
as
\eta
and
\zeta
unless there are confusions or
we want to emphasis they are twists instead of arcs.
Then equations in (
2.3
) becomes
\Psi(a)=a^{\underline{\Psi}}
for
a=\eta
or
a=\zeta
.

\partial_{1}
\partial_{b}
\partial_{2}
\cdots

\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\Delta})

In particular, a set of generators of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is shown in
Figure

,
which consists of braid twists along
\sigma_{i}
and L-twist along
\delta_{r}
.
Set

(2.4)

\operatorname{rk}\colon=2g+b+p-1.

Let

(2.5)

\zeta_{s}\colon=\delta_{2g+b-1+s},\quad 1\leq s\leq p

be the L-arc encloses the puncture
P_{s}
.

2.2.
Mixed twist groups

In
[
Qy2
]
, we introduce a subgroup
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
called
braid twist group
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
, which
is generated by the braid twist
B_{\eta}
for any
\eta\in\operatorname{CA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
In this paper, we shall introduce yet another class of twist groups,
that sits between the two groups above.

Let
\leftmoon\subset\text{\Sun}
be a subset of punctures
and
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)
be the set of L-arcs consisting of the one that encloses exactly one puncture
P_{i}
in
\leftmoon
.

For instance,
\delta_{2g+b+s-1}
is in
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)
if and only if
P_{s}\in\leftmoon
,
for any
1\leq s\leq p
in
Figure

3
.
In fact, we have

\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)=\{\Psi(\zeta_{s})\mid P_{s}\in\leftmoon,\Psi\in\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\}.

On the one hand,
\Psi\in\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
preserves punctures pointwise.
Hence
\Psi(\zeta_{s})
encloses the exactly the same puncture
P_{s}
as
\zeta_{s}
does.
On the other hand, for any
\delta
in
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)
that encloses the puncture
P_{s}
,
one can find a mapping class that maps
\delta
to
\zeta_{s}
, which confirms the claim.
Let

(2.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}(\leftmoon)\colon=\langle L_{\zeta}\mid\zeta\in\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)\rangle

be the normal subgroup of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta})
generated L-twist along the L-arcs in
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\leftmoon)
.

Definition 2.1
.

The
mixed twist group

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})=\langle\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}),\operatorname{L}(\leftmoon)\rangle
is defined to be
the (normal) subgroup of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
generated by L-twists in
\operatorname{L}(\leftmoon)
and all braid twists.

Take a sequence of subsets

\varnothing=\leftmoon_{0}\subset\leftmoon_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\leftmoon_{j}\subset\cdots\subset\leftmoon_{p}=\text{\Sun}

with
\leftmoon_{j}=\{P_{1},\cdots,P_{j}\}
.
It induces a normal series/subnormal series

\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{0}})\lhd\cdots\lhd\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})\lhd\cdots\lhd\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{p}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\lhd\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}).

In fact, by the conjugation formula,
we know that any former group in the series is a normal subgroup of a latter one.
These are essentially all the different mixed twist groups we will study.

Example 2.2
.

When
\mathbf{S}
is a disk with
\text{\Sun}=\varnothing
,
the only mixed twist groups equals
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta})
,
which is the classical braid group
\operatorname{Br}_{w}
of type
A_{w-1}
.

When
\mathbf{S}
is disk and
p=1
, then
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is isomorphic to braid group of affine type
A_{w-1}

and is strictly smaller than
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
, which is isomorphic to braid group of type
B_{w}
.

2.3.
Topological Abel-Jacobi maps

Let
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon})=\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon},\mathbb{Z})
be the first homology of
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon}
.
Forgetting about punctures in
\leftmoon
gives a map
F^{\leftmoon}\colon\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}}
and induces a short exact sequence

1\to\mathbb{Z}^{\leftmoon}\to\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{F^{\leftmoon}_{*}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})\to 1.

We will write
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(?)
for the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{?}
(indexed by
?
).

For any element
\zeta
in
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
there are
w
paths (or strings in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{conf}_{w}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
)

\{p_{i}\colon Z_{i}\to\zeta(Z_{i})\mid Z_{i}\in\Delta\}

on
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
, which forms some numbers of cycles in
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
Taking the union/product of these cycles gives an element in
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
which define a map

(2.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}\colon\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

sending
\zeta
to
[\coprod p_{i}]
.

Definition 2.3
.

We define the
(topological) Abel-Jacobi=AJ map relative to
\leftmoon
as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}=F^{\leftmoon}_{*}\circ\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}\colon\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}}).

In particular, we have two special ones:
\operatorname{AJ}=\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\text{\Sun}}
and
\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}=\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\varnothing}
.

In the remaining of the section, we prove that the mixed twist groups are
exactly the kernels of the corresponding relative AJ maps and give finite presentations of which.

2.4.
The kernels of the relative AJ maps

We first recall a finite presentation of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Theorem 2.4

(Bellingeri-Godelle)
.

There is a finite presentation of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
with

•

generators
\sigma_{1},\cdots,\sigma_{w-1}
and
\delta_{1},\cdots\delta_{\operatorname{rk}}
;

•

relations

–

\operatorname{Co}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})
, i.e.
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}=\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}
,  if
|i-j|=1
;

–

\operatorname{Br}(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j})
, i.e.
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}=\sigma_{j}\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}
,  if
|i-j|>1
;;

–

\operatorname{Co}(\delta_{r},\sigma_{1}\delta_{r}\sigma_{1})
;

–

\operatorname{Co}(\sigma_{i},\delta_{r})
,  if
i\neq 1
;

–

\operatorname{Co}(\delta_{s}^{\sigma_{1}},\delta_{r})
,
if
s<r
and
s+1\notin 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}
;

–

\sigma_{1}\delta_{r}\sigma_{1}\delta_{r-1}\sigma_{1}=\delta_{r-1}\sigma_{1}\delta_{r}
,
if
r+1\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}
.

Here
2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}
is the set of positive even (resp. odd) integer not bigger than
2g
.

For consentience, we introduce the
\varepsilon_{r}
inductively as alternative generators
(set
\varepsilon_{0}=1
):

(2.9)

\varepsilon_{r}=\begin{cases}\delta_{r}\varepsilon_{r-1}&\text{if $r\notin 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$,}\\
\delta_{r}\varepsilon_{r-2}&\text{if $r\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$,}\end{cases}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\delta_{r}=\begin{cases}\varepsilon_{r}\underline{\varepsilon_{r-1}}&\text{if $r\notin 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$,}\\
\varepsilon_{r}\underline{\varepsilon_{r-2}}&\text{if $r\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$,}\end{cases}

for
1\leq r\leq\operatorname{rk}
.

For
1\leq s<r\leq\operatorname{rk}
, the relative position of
\varepsilon_{s}
and
\varepsilon_{r}

are shown in
Figure

4
depending on
s+1\notin 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}
(left picture)
or
s+1\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}
(right picture).
Let

(2.10)

\displaystyle\tau_{r}=\sigma_{1}^{\underline{\varepsilon_{r}}},\quad 1\leq r\leq\operatorname{rk}.

Take any decoration other than
Z_{1}/Z_{2}
and two closed arcs
a,b
as shown there.
For instance, one can take
a=\sigma_{1}^{\underline{\sigma_{2}}},b=\sigma_{2}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

For
1\leq s<r\leq\operatorname{rk}
, the commutator

(2.11)

\displaystyle[\varepsilon_{s},\varepsilon_{r}]=\begin{cases}\underline{\tau_{s}b}a\underline{\tau_{r}a\tau_{s}}ab\tau_{r}b&\text{if $s+1\notin 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$},\\
\underline{bb\tau_{s}b}a\tau_{r}\underline{a}\tau_{s}ab\tau_{r}b&\text{if $s+1\in 2\mathbb{Z}^{+}_{\leq g}$},\end{cases}

is in fact in
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\Delta})
. Denote the inverse of the right hand side of (

.

\quad\varepsilon_{s}

Proof.

To examine the mapping classes, we can restrict to the neighbourhood of the union of arcs

A straightforward checking that

This is again an easy picture-chasing:

In the former case, one can rewrite the right hand side as (using

it finally moves along

and one sees that the claim holds.∎

Note that the calculation above (and the theorem below) is essentially hidden in
[

]
.

Let

(2.12)

\displaystyle H^{\leftmoon}\colon=\pi_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon},Z_{1})=\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon}_{Z_{1}})\leq\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}),

which can be identified with a subgroup of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\Delta})
generated by
\zeta_{s}
for
P_{s}\in\leftmoon
.
Moreover, one has
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon})=H^{\leftmoon}\big/[H^{\leftmoon},H^{\leftmoon}]
.

Theorem 2.6
.

There is a short exact sequence

(2.13)

\displaystyle 1\to\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})\to\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\xrightarrow{\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}}\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})\to 1,

or equivalently
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}
.
Moreover,
H^{{\rightmoon}}\cap\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})=[H^{{\rightmoon}},H^{{\rightmoon}}]
.

Proof.

We write
N=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})=\langle\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}),\zeta_{s}\mid s\in\leftmoon\rangle,H=H^{{\rightmoon}}
in this proof.
As
\{\sigma_{i},\delta_{j}\}
is a generating set,
one deduces that
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=N\cdot H
.
Hence we have a short exact sequence

(2.14)

1\to N\to\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to H\big/H\cap N\to 1

or
H\big/H\cap N=\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/N
.

Clearly,
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\subset\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
This is because any braid twist
B_{\eta}
corresponds to two paths
p_{i}

(i.e. going along
\eta
one way or the other),
which are inverse to each other and form a trivial/contractible cycle.
As
\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}=F_{*}^{\leftmoon}\circ\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}
, we have

N\subset\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}\lhd\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}
.
Also,
\zeta_{s}\in\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}
for
P_{s}\in\leftmoon
as such a puncture is forgotten in
F^{\leftmoon}
.
Therefore the map
\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}
factors through the quotient by (the intersection with)
N
:

(2.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(=H\big/[H,H]).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\big/H\cap N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\leftmoon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Xi}

Restricted to
H
, we see that
H\cap N\subset[H,H]
.

One the other hand, by
Lemma

2.5
,
[\varepsilon_{r},\varepsilon_{s}]
is in
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\lhd N
for any
\varepsilon_{r},\varepsilon_{s}\in H
.
As they (normally) generated
[H,H]
, it implies that
H\cap N\supset[H,H]
and thus
H\cap N=[H,H]
.
Looking back at (
2.15
), we see that
\Xi
is an isomorphism and
(
2.14
) becomes the required (
2.13
).
∎

In particular, we have

(2.16)

\begin{cases}\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\varnothing})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\varnothing},\\
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\text{\Sun}})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}^{\text{\Sun}}_{\text{\Sun}}=\ker\operatorname{AJ}\end{cases}

and a commutative diagram of short exact sequences of groups (which will be called an octahedron)
show as the left diagram of
Figure

6
.
The right graph of
Figure

6
is the Auslander-Reiten quiver style view (of the octahedron among more short exact sequences).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}

\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})

\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{{\rightmoon}})

An octahedron (left) and an AR-quiver style view (right)

2.5.
Generators from dual graph of a decorated triangulation

Recall that an open arc in
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
is a curve connected points in
\mathbf{M}\cup\text{\Sun}
.
A
triangulation

\mathrm{T}=\{\gamma_{i}\}
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
is a maximal collection of compatible (i.e. no intersection in the interior of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
) open arcs
\gamma_{i}
, which will divide
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
into triangles
(called
\mathrm{T}
-triangle
or just a triangle for short sometimes).
The following is a well-known fact.

Lemma 2.7
.

Any triangulation
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
consists of

(2.17)

\displaystyle n=\operatorname{rank}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=6g+3p+3b+m-6

(simple essential) open arcs that divides
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
in to
({2n+m})/{3}
triangles.

From now on, we assume that the DMSp
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
satisfies

(2.18)

w=\frac{2n+m}{3}=4g+2p+2b+m-4\geq 1.

Since
m,b\geq 1
and
g\geq 0
, then
w\geq 2p-1
.

We associated a quiver with potential to any (decoration) triangulations.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathrm{T}
be a triangulation of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
Define the (degenerate when
\text{\Sun}\neq\varnothing
) quiver with potential
(Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}},W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}})
as follows:

•

The vertices of
Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
are indexed by open arcs in
\mathrm{T}
.

•

The arrow of
Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
correspond to clockwise angles between edges of
\mathrm{T}
-triangle
T
.
Hence, there is a 3-cycle
Q_{T}
sub-quiver in
(Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}},W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}})
for each
T
.

•

The potential
W_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
are the sum over all 3-cycle over all
\mathrm{T}
-triangle
T
,
which will be called the 3-cycles potential.

See
Figure

7
for example.
By definition, the (forward) flip of triangulations induces
the (forward) mutation of the associated quivers with potential.

Figure 7.
Quivers with 3-cycles potential associated to triangulations

A
\mathrm{T}
-triangle
T
is
self-folded
if two of its edges are coincide as one
\gamma_{i}
.
We call such a
\gamma_{i}
a
self-folded edge
.
The other edge of
T
is call an
enclosing edge
.

Definition 2.9
.

A puncture is called
\mathrm{T}
-isolated
, if it is enclosed in a self-folded
\mathrm{T}
-triangle.
Denote by
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})
the set of
\mathrm{T}
-isolated triangles.
We say a triangulation
\mathrm{T}
is
admissible
if all punctures are
\mathrm{T}
-isolated, i.e.
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})=\text{\Sun}
.

Note that the quiver
Q_{\mathrm{T}}^{\text{\Sun}}
has exactly
|\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})|
loops and
|\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})|
2-cycles,

For any decorated triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
,
the associated quiver with potential
(Q_{\mathbb{T}},W_{\mathbb{T}})

is defined to the one associated to
\mathrm{T}=F^{\Delta}(\mathbb{T})
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
.
Then we have the same notions of
\mathbb{T}
-isolated,
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})
and admissible in the decorated case.

Let
\mathbb{T}^{*}
be the dual graph of
\mathbb{T}
consisting of closed arcs.
There is a partition
\mathbb{T}^{*}=\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}\sqcup\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
for

\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}=\mathbb{T}^{*}\cap\operatorname{CA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}=\mathbb{T}^{*}\cap\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}).

Note that
\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
are precisely the dual of self-folded edges in
\mathbb{T}
.

Convention 2.10
.

For
Q_{\mathbb{T}}
, we will identify its vertices with (simple or L-) closed arcs.

Definition 2.11
.

The
mixed twist group

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
with respect to a triangulation
\mathbb{T}
is defined to be
the subgroup of
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
generated by the braid twists
\eta
for
\eta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
and
the L-twists
\zeta
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
.

By
[
QZ1
, Lem. A.1]
,
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
(and hence
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
admits admissible triangulations.
Assume
\mathbb{T}
is admissible.
Then by cutting out all the self-folded edges in self-folded
\mathbb{T}
-triangles,
we obtain a triangulation
\mathbb{T}_{0}
for the result DMS
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{0}
without punctures.
Moreover, one can naturally identify arcs in
\mathbb{T}_{\operatorname{CA}}
and arcs in
\mathbb{T}_{0}

as well as their dual graphs
\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}=\mathbb{T}_{0}^{*}
.

By
[
QZ2
, Thm. 5.9]
, we have a presentation of

\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{0})=\operatorname{BT}(\mathbb{T}_{0})
with
\mathbb{T}_{0}^{*}
as the set of generators and
a set of relations, denote by
R(\mathbb{T}_{0})
,
described in terms of the associated quiver with potential
(Q_{\mathbb{T}_{0}},W_{\mathbb{T}_{0}})
.
Here,
(Q_{\mathbb{T}_{0}},W_{\mathbb{T}_{0}})
is the full sub-quiver of
(Q_{\mathbb{T}},W_{\mathbb{T}})
, restricted to
Q_{\mathbb{T}_{\operatorname{CA}}}=Q_{\mathbb{T}_{0}}

and
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbb{T}_{0})
can be naturally embedded into
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
(induced from the embedding
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
\mathbb{T}
be an adimissible triangulation of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
and

\mathbb{T}_{0}
the corresponding triangulation of the surface
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{0}
cutting at the punctures in
\leftmoon(\mathbb{T})
.
Then the mixed twist group
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
has the following finite presentation:

•

Generators:

–

generators of
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbb{T}_{0})
(i.e. braid twists
B_{\eta}
for
\eta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
) together with

–

L-twists
L_{\zeta}
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
.

•

Relations:

–

R(\mathbb{T}_{0})
for
\operatorname{BT}(\mathbb{T}_{0})
together with

–

\operatorname{Br}^{2\operatorname{Int}(\beta,\zeta)+2}(\beta,\zeta)
for any
\beta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}
and
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
.

A special case of the theorem above is the following (when
Q_{\mathbb{T}}
has no double arrows),
where the relations are much simpler.

Corollary 2.13
.

For an admissible triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
such that there is no double arrow in
Q_{\mathbb{T}}
,
then the mixed twist group
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
has the following finite presentation:

•

Generators: braid twists
\eta
for
\eta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
and
L-twists
\zeta
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
.

•

Relations:

–

higher braid relation
\operatorname{Br}^{2\operatorname{Int}(a,b)+2}(a,b)
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{T}^{*}
.

–

triangle relation
abca=bcab=cabc
if
a,b,c\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
share a common endpoint/decoration
Z
and they are in clockwise order at
Z
.

Note that for the (higher) braid relation in
Corollary

2.13
, there are three cases:

•

\operatorname{Co}(a,b)
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{T}^{*}
with
\operatorname{Int}(a,b)=0
.

•

\operatorname{Br}(a,b)
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
with
\operatorname{Int}(a,b)=1/2
.

•

\operatorname{Br}^{4}(a,b)
for
a\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
and
b\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
with
\operatorname{Int}(a,b)=1
.

Here
\operatorname{Int}
is the intersection number, cf.
[
Qy2
, Def. 3.1]
.

2.6.
Proof of
Theorem

2.12

Generators

We first show that
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j+1}})=\langle\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}}),\zeta_{j}\rangle
,
which follows from the following lemma.

Lemma 2.14
.

If two L-arcs
\zeta_{1}
and
\zeta_{2}
in
\operatorname{LA}(\leftmoon)
enclosing the same puncture
P
,
then there is
b\in\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
such that
\zeta_{1}=b(\zeta_{2})
.
In particular,
L_{\zeta_{2}}=L_{\zeta_{1}}^{b}
.

Proof.

An L-arc
\zeta
in
\operatorname{LA}(\leftmoon)
cut out a once-puncture disk,
which contains a unique simple arc connecting the puncture inside and the decoration (its endpoint).
Call such an arc the bone of
\zeta
. Denote by
g_{i}
the bone of
\zeta_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Then we only need to show that there exist
b\in\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
such that
b(g_{2})=b(g_{1})
.

We choose a triangulation
\mathbb{T}
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
such that
g_{i}
is contained in a triangle.
This is can be done easily due to change of coordinate principal.
Then we have

\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},g_{1})=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathbb{T}}\operatorname{Int}_{\mathbf{S}^{\circ}}(\gamma,g_{1})=0.

By (the proof of)
[
Qy2
, Lem. 3.14]
,
there exist
b^{\prime}\in\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
such that
\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},b^{\prime}(g_{2}))<\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},g_{2})
unless
\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},g_{2})=0

By induction on
\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},g_{i})
, there exists
b\in\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
such that
\operatorname{Int}(\mathbb{T},b(g_{2}))=0
,
which implies that
b(g_{2})=g_{1}
.
∎

An inductive algorithm

Lemma 2.15
.

Suppose that there is a finite presentation

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})=\frac{\langle a,\eta,x,\cdots\rangle}{\Big(\operatorname{Br}(\eta,x),\operatorname{Br}(a,x),\operatorname{Br}(a,x^{\eta}),\operatorname{Co}(?,a),\operatorname{Co}(?,\eta),?\neq x\Big)\bigcup R_{j}},

where
\eta,x,a
are shown in
Figure

8
,
the rest of the generators are disjoint with
\eta\cup a
and
any relation in
R_{j}
does not involve
\eta
and
a
.

Then there is a finite presentation

(2.19)

\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j+1}})=\frac{\langle\zeta,\eta,x,\cdots\rangle}{\Big(\operatorname{Br}^{4}(\zeta,\eta),\operatorname{Br}(\eta,x),\operatorname{Co}(x,\zeta),\operatorname{Co}(?,\zeta),\operatorname{Co}(?,\eta),?\neq x\Big)\bigcup R_{j}}

for some conjugation
\zeta
of
\zeta_{j+1}
.

Proof.

We can make the (inner) semidirect product
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathbb{Z}\langle\zeta\rangle
that fits into the following commutative diagram of short exact sequences of groups:

(2.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})\rtimes_{\rho}\mathbb{Z}\langle\zeta\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle\zeta\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j+1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle\zeta\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

By calculating the conjugation action of generators by
\zeta
(denoted by
\rho_{\zeta}
),
e.g.

\rho_{\zeta}(\eta)=a^{\prime}=a^{\underline{\eta}}\quad\text{gives relation}\quad\eta^{\zeta}=a^{\underline{\eta}},

we deduce that
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon_{j}})
admits a presentation with generators

\langle\zeta,a,\eta,x,\cdots\rangle
and relations

(2.21)

\Big(\begin{cases}\operatorname{Br}(\eta,x),\operatorname{Br}(a,x),\operatorname{Br}(a,x^{\eta}),\operatorname{Co}(?,a),\operatorname{Co}(?,\eta),?\neq x\\
a^{\zeta}=\eta,\eta^{\zeta}=a^{\underline{\eta}},x^{\zeta}=x,\operatorname{Co}(*,\zeta),*\neq\eta,a,x\end{cases}\Big)\bigcup R_{j}.

Here, for most conjugations by
\rho_{\zeta}
, the element
*
(e.g.
x
) does not change
so that we get
\operatorname{Co}(*,\zeta)
.
By
a^{\zeta}=\eta\Leftrightarrow a=\eta^{\underline{\zeta}}
, we can eliminate
a
from the generating set and direct calculation shows the following

(2.22)

\displaystyle\eta^{\zeta}=a^{\underline{\eta}}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\underline{\zeta}\eta\zeta=\eta\eta^{\underline{\zeta}}\underline{\eta}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\zeta\eta\zeta\eta=\eta\zeta\eta\zeta

or
\operatorname{Br}^{4}(\zeta,\eta)
as expected.
Furthermore, using these relation, we have

\begin{cases}\operatorname{Br}(a,x)\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta^{\underline{\zeta}},x)\;{\mathrel{\to}^{\operatorname{Co}(\zeta,x)}}\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta,x)\\
\operatorname{Br}(a,x^{\eta})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta^{\underline{\zeta}},x^{\eta})\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta^{\underline{\zeta\eta}},x)\;{\mathrel{\to}^{\operatorname{Br}^{4}(\zeta,\eta)}}\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta^{\zeta},x)\;{\mathrel{\to}^{\operatorname{Co}(\zeta,x)}}\;\operatorname{Br}(\eta,x).\end{cases}

Thus, the presentation (
2.21
) simplifies to (
2.19
).
∎

\zeta
a
a^{\prime}
\bullet
\circ
\eta
\bullet
\circ
x
\bullet
\circ
\blacksquare
