The
short dumbbell (S-DB) relation
(at
\mathrm{T}
with respect to
i,j
) is the hexagonal relations shown in (
4.3
),
or
x^{2}y=yx^{2}
staring at
\mathrm{T}
.
Let
t_{i}=yx
and
t_{j}=xy
be the
flip twists (i.e. 2-cycle)
at
\mathrm{T}
and
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
, respectively.
Then the relation can be written as conjugation relation
t_{j}=t_{i}^{x}
.

4.2.
Extra relations in the punctured case

Figure 13.
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
for a once-punctured digon as local flip graph

Given a triangulation
\mathrm{T}
in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
with two vertices
i,j
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}

such that they are in a digon as shown in the middle picture of
Figure

13
.
They there is a full subgraph of
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
as shown there.
We name three triangulations
\mathrm{T}_{L},\mathrm{T}_{M}
and
\mathrm{T}_{R}
, respectively (cf.
Figure

13
and the third picture of
Figure

14
).
We also name usual flips/edges
u,v
and L-flips/loop edges
s,r
as shown there,
where the color matches the open arc that they flip at.

Long dumbbell (L-DB) relations

The
long dumbbell relations
(at
\mathrm{T}_{M}
with respect to
i,j
)
are

•

r=s^{uv}
(
\Leftrightarrow suv=uvr
) at
\mathrm{T}_{L}
and

•

s=r^{vu}
(
\Leftrightarrow vus=rvu
) at
\mathrm{T}_{R}
,

as shown in the left two picture of
Figure

14
.

Figure 14.
Two L-DB and a Sym-Hex

Symmetric hexagon (Sym-Hex) relations

Continue the set up as above (for long dumbbell relation).

The
symmetric hexagon relations
(at
\mathrm{T}_{M}
with respect to
i,j
) is
usu=vrv
,
as shown in the right picture of
Figure

14
.
Note that they will also play a role in the conjugation formula later.

Definition 4.1
.

The
flip groupoid

\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
is the quotient of
the path groupoid of
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

by all (square/pentagon, S-DB, L-DB, Sym-Hex) relations above.

These extra relations have been observed by some experts, cf.
[
Hai
,
HKQ
,
IN
]
.

4.3.
Covering groupoids

Similar to
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, we have the groupoid version of
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Figure 15.
Higher braid relation
\eta\delta\eta\delta=\delta\eta\delta\eta
decomposes into 4 hexagons

Definition 4.2
.

There are the following lifts in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
:

•

A square is shown in the left picture of
Figure

12
.

•

A pentagon is shown in the middle picture of
Figure

12
.

•

An S-DB is shown in the right picture of
Figure

12
.

•

Two L-DB are shown as the top-left/bottom-right hexagons in
Figure

15
.

•

Two Sym-Hex are shown as the top-right/bottom-left hexagons in
Figure

15
.

The
flip groupoid

\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is the quotient of
the path groupoid of
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
by the five kinds of relations above.

As the relations in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
lifts to relations in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
the isomorphism in
Theorem

3.10
upgrades to an isomorphism between groupoids:

F^{\Delta}_{*}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{MT}^{\text{\Sun}}\cong\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}).

4.4.
Local flip twists

There are two types
(local) flip twists
at a triangulation
\mathrm{T}\in\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
:
the
2-cycle (twist)

t_{i}
corresponding flipping twice a non self-folded edge
i
of
\mathrm{T}

(as mentioned above) and the
loop (twist)

l_{s}=s
corresponding flipping once a self-folded edge
s
of
\mathrm{T}
.
In total, there are
n
flip twists at each
\mathbb{T}
.

Definition 4.3
.

Define the
flip twist group

\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
to be
the subgroup of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
generated by flip twists at
\mathrm{T}
.

Lemma 4.4
.

The following (higher) braid relations hold in any local flip group
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
:

•

\operatorname{Co}(t_{i},t_{j})
between two 2-cycles if there is no arrow between
i
and
j
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
.

•

\operatorname{Br}(t_{i},t_{j})
between two 2-cycles if there is exactly 1 arrow between
i
and
j
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
.

•

\operatorname{Co}(l_{s},l_{r})
between two loops if there is no arrow between
s
and
r
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
.

•

\operatorname{Co}(t_{i},l_{s})
between a 2-cycle and a loop if there is no arrow between
i
and
s
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
.

•

\operatorname{Br}^{4}(t_{i},l_{s})
between a 2-cycle and a loop if there are two arrows between
i
and
s
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}

(that forms a two-cycle).

Proof.

The first two relations are shown in
[
KQ2
, Lem. 2.7, cf. Fig. 2]
.
The next two commutation relations follow the same way as the first commutation relation,
(as the flips are apart).

We only need to check the last higher braid relation.
It follows by assembling 2 long dumbbells and 2 symmetric hexagons
as shown in the left picture of
Figure

17
.
More precisely, at
\mathbb{T}_{s}
in
Figures

14
and
14
, we have
l_{s}=s
and
t_{i}=u^{2}
.
The corresponding algebraic calculation is:

\begin{array}[]{rl}suusuu=&(suv)\cdot\underline{v}\cdot(usu)\cdot u\\
=&(uvr)\cdot\underline{v}\cdot(vrv)\cdot u\\
=&uvrrvu\\
=&u\cdot(vrv)\cdot\underline{v}\cdot(rvu)\\
=&u\cdot(usu)\cdot\underline{v}\cdot(vus)=uusuu.\end{array}\qed

Remark 4.5
.

The commutation, braid and higher braid relations decomposes into 4 squares, 6 pentagons and 4 hexagons, respectively.

Figure

16
and
Figure

15
shows the lifting of braid and higher braid relations in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
, respectively.

Figure 16.
Braid relation decomposes into 6 pentagons

Figure 17.
Two ways of decomposing of higher braid relation
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
.

Left: 2 L-DB and 2 Sym-Hex; Right: 8 Non-Sym-Hex

Remark 4.6

(Alternative decomposition of
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
via non-symmetric hexagon relations)
.

There is another type of (non–symmetric) hexagon relation, appearing in the cluster braid groupoids of type
I_{2}(4)=B_{2}=C_{2}
Dynkin diagrams,
see the picture below.

(4.4)

\bullet
x
y
\bullet
x
y
\bullet
x
y
\bullet
x
y
\bullet
x
y
\bullet
x
y
\otimes
\mathrm{T}=\;
\odot
\Big/_{x^{2}=y^{4}}
.

These relations clearly different from our new long dumbbell/symmetric hexagon relations.
Nevertheless, they both produce
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
relation in different fashion.
See the right picture of
Figure

17
that
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
relation decomposes into 8 non-symmetric hexagons
(which is the same style as
Figure

16
).

In general, there are polygonal (i.e.
(h+2)
-gon) relations (cf.
[
HHQ
]
) of the form
x^{2}=y^{h}
,
which essentially come from weighted folding for type
I_{2}(h)
quiver/diagram
and produces
\operatorname{Br}^{h}
relations.

Recall that we have chosen an initial triangulation
\mathbb{T}\in\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

By (
3.11
), we have a short exact sequence:

(4.5)

1\to\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},\mathbb{T})\to\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}},\mathrm{T})\xrightarrow{i_{\mathrm{T}}}\operatorname{MT}^{\text{\Sun}}\to 1

for
\mathrm{T}=F_{*}^{\Delta}(\mathbb{T})
.
By formula (
3.9
) and (
3.10
), there is a natural homomorphism

(4.6)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}i_{\mathrm{T}}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})&\twoheadrightarrow&\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})\;\big(\lhd\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\lhd\operatorname{MT}^{\text{\Sun}}\big)\\
t_{i}&\mapsto&B_{\eta},\\
l_{j}&\mapsto&L_{\delta},\end{array}

for
\eta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{CA}}
dual to a non self-fold arc in
\mathrm{T}
that corresponds to vertex
i
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
and,
for
\delta\in\mathbb{T}^{*}_{\operatorname{LA}}
dual to a self-fold arc in
\mathrm{T}
that corresponds to vertex
j
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}}
.

5.
Properties of flip twist groups

We study relations between flip twist groups associated to different triangulations
and their relations with the corresponding mixed twist groups in this section.

5.1.
Conjugation formulae

2-cycle twist groups

Let
\mathrm{T}
be a triangulation of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
and
P\in\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})
a
\mathrm{T}
-isolated puncture,
locally shown as
\mathrm{T}_{L}
in
Figure

13
.
There is a loop twist
s
associated to the self-folded edge (or vertex
2
there)
and a 2-cycle twist
u^{2}
associated to the enclosing edge.
Call the conjugated twist

(u^{2})^{\underline{s}}=su\cdot u\underline{s}{\mathrel{\to}^{usu=vrv}}\underline{u}vrv\cdot u\underline{s}{\mathrel{\to}^{r=s^{\underline{uv}}}}\underline{u}vvu=(v^{2})^{u}

the
2-cycle (twist)
associated to
the loop
s
at
\mathrm{T}
of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
Then to each triangulation of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, there are
n
associated 2-cycle twists.

Definition 5.1
.

The 2-cycle twist group
\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T})
is the subgroup of
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
generated by
all 2-cycle twists at
\mathrm{T}
.

Twist groups related by a flip

We now study the relationship between the flip twist groups of triangulations that differ by a flip.
Recall that
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T})
denotes the set of punctures that are enclosed in some self-folded
\mathrm{T}
-triangles.

Consider a pair of forward flips between two triangulations (which may coincide):

\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}
\colon\mu_{\bullet}
\mathrm{T}_{\circ}
\mu_{\circ}\colon
i
z_{\circ}
z_{\bullet}

in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
at arcs
z_{\circ}
and
z_{\bullet}
respectively.
Denote by
i
the corresponding vertex in
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}
and
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}}
.
Note that
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})
and
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})
differ by at most one puncture.
Without loss of generality, suppose that
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})\subset\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})
.
The flip twist of
\mathrm{T}_{*}
with respect to a vertex
i
of
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{*}}
will be denoted by
t^{*}_{i}
or
l^{*}_{i}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

[Conjugation formulae]

•

If the flip
\mu_{i}
is a loop in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, i.e. as

\mu_{\circ}=\mu_{2}=s
at
\mathrm{T}_{\circ}=\mathrm{T}_{L}=\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}
in
Figure

13
.
Then there is a conjugation/isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{ad}_{\mu_{\circ}}=\operatorname{ad}_{s}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})&\to&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}),\\
f&\mapsto&f^{s}.\end{array}

•

If
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})=\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})
and
\mathrm{T}_{\circ}\neq\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}
, then
there is a natural isomorphism

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{ad}_{\mu_{\circ}}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})&\xrightarrow{\cong}&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}),\\
t^{\circ}_{i}&\mapsto&t^{\bullet}_{i},\\
l^{\circ}_{j}&\mapsto&l^{\bullet}_{j},\\
t^{\circ}_{k}&\mapsto&\underline{t^{\bullet}_{i}}t^{\bullet}_{k}t^{\bullet}_{i},\end{array}

for any
j
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}
corresponding to a self-fold arc
for any
k\neq i
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}
corresponding to a non self-fold arc.
More precisely, one has
\operatorname{ad}_{\mu_{\circ}}(t^{\circ}_{k})=t^{\bullet}_{k}
if and only if there is no arrow
from
k
to
i
in
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{\circ}}
.

•

If
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})=\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})\cup\{P\}
, i.e. as

(\mathrm{T}_{\circ},\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})=(\mathrm{T}_{M},\mathrm{T}_{L})
and
\mu_{\circ}=v,\mu_{\bullet}=u
in
Figure

13
.
Then there is an injection

(5.1)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{ad}_{\mu_{\circ}}=\operatorname{ad}_{v}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{M})&\to&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{L}),\\
t^{M}_{1}&\mapsto&t^{L}_{1},\\
t^{M}_{2}&\mapsto&l^{L}_{2}t^{L}_{1}\underline{l^{L}_{2}},\\
t^{M}_{3}&\mapsto&\underline{t^{L}_{1}}t^{L}_{3}t^{L}_{1},\\
f^{M}_{k}&\mapsto&f^{L}_{k},\end{array}

with vertices
1,2,3
of
Q_{\mathrm{T}_{M}}
shown in
Figure

13
and any other vertex
k
with flip twist
f^{?}_{k}
in
\mathrm{T}_{?}
.
In fact, we have
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{L})=\langle\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{M})^{\mu},l_{2}^{L}\rangle
for
l_{2}^{L}=s
.

When restricted to
\operatorname{CT}
, we always have a natural isomorphism

(5.2)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{ad}_{\mu_{\circ}}\colon\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})&\to&\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet}),\\
f&\mapsto&f^{s}.\end{array}

Proof.

For the first case, the formula for
\operatorname{ad}_{\mu}(t_{z})
follows from the higher braid relation
\operatorname{Br}^{4}(s,u)

as we have seen in
Figure

17
.

For the second case, it follows essentially the same way as
[
KQ2
, Prop. 2.8]
.
The only difference is that for
j
corresponding to a self-fold arc,
there is no edge between
j
and
i
– otherwise
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\circ})\neq\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{\bullet})
.
Hence
(l^{\circ}_{j})^{\mu_{i}}=l^{\bullet}_{j}
is a square-commutation relation.

For the third case,
we have

•

For
1
,
(t^{M}_{1})^{u}=(u^{2})^{u}=u^{2}=t^{L}_{1}
.

•

For
2
,
(t^{M}_{2})^{u}=(v^{2})^{u}=\underline{u}v^{2}u
and
l^{L}_{2}t^{L}_{1}\underline{l^{L}_{2}}=su^{2}\underline{s}
.
Then

usuu{\mathrel{\to}^{usu=vrv}}vrvu{\mathrel{\to}^{r=s^{\underline{uv}}}}vvus

(or the combination of the two hexagon relations on the right in
Figure

15
)
implies that
(t^{M}_{2})^{u}=l^{L}_{2}t^{L}_{1}\underline{l^{L}_{2}}
.

•

For
3
,
(t^{M}_{3})^{u}=\underline{t^{L}_{2}}t^{L}_{3}t^{L}_{2}
follows the same way as in the second case.

•

For
k=4
,
(t^{M}_{4})^{u}=t^{L}_{4}
is an S-DB relation.

•

For other
k
,
(f^{M}_{k})^{u}=f^{L}_{k}
is a square=commutation relation (as there is no arrows between
i=2
and
k
).∎

Note that the flip twist groups are NOT all isomorphic to each other by (
5.1
).

As a consequence, the 2-cycle twist groups are all isomorphic to each other.
In fact, a more precise statement can be made as follows.

Corollary 5.3
.

\{\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T})\mid\mathrm{T}\in\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\}
is a normal subgroupoid of
\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Proof.

This is the analogue of the result of first paragraph
[
KQ2
, Prop. 2.9]
,
which follows the same way since we have the conjugation isomorphisms (
5.2
).
∎

The
\lozenge
-flips

Definition 5.4
.

[
FST
]

A (forward)
\lozenge
-flip is the composition of locally flipping twice at a enclosing edge of a self-folded triangle of a triangulation
\mathrm{T}
of
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider a pair of
\lozenge
-flips

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces uv}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces vu}

between admissible triangulations as in
Figure

13
.
Then there is a natural isomorphism

(5.3)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{ad}_{uv}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{L})&\to&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{R}),\\
t^{L}_{1}&\mapsto&l^{R}_{1}t^{R}_{2}\underline{l^{R}_{1}},\\
l^{L}_{2}&\mapsto&l^{R}_{1},\\
t^{L}_{4}&\mapsto&(t^{R}_{4})^{t^{R}_{2}l^{R}_{1}t^{R}_{2}\underline{l^{R}_{1}}},\\
f^{L}_{k}&\mapsto&f^{R}_{k},\end{array}

Proof.

Applying (
5.1
) twice, we have

(5.4)

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{L})&\xleftarrow{(-)^{u}}&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{M})&\xrightarrow{(-)^{v}}&\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{R}),\\
t^{L}_{1}&\mapsfrom&t^{M}_{1}&\mapsto&l^{R}_{1}t^{R}_{2}\underline{l^{R}_{1}},\\
l^{L}_{2}t^{L}_{1}\underline{l^{L}_{2}}&\mapsfrom&t^{M}_{2}&\mapsto&t^{R}_{2},\\
\underline{t^{L}_{1}}t^{L}_{3}t^{L}_{1}&\mapsfrom&t^{M}_{3}&\mapsto&t^{R}_{3},\\
t^{L}_{4}&\mapsfrom&t^{M}_{4}&\mapsto&\underline{t^{R}_{2}}t^{R}_{4}t^{R}_{2},\\
f^{L}_{k}&\mapsfrom&f^{M}_{k}&\mapsto&f^{R}_{k},\\
\end{array}

Thus, we deduce that

\begin{cases}(t^{L}_{1})^{uv}=\big((t^{L}_{1})^{\underline{u}}\big)^{u^{2}v}=(t^{M}_{1})^{u^{2}v}{\mathrel{\to}^{u^{2}=t^{M}_{1}}}(t^{M}_{1})^{v}=l^{R}_{1}t^{R}_{2}\underline{l^{R}_{1}},\\
(l^{L}_{2})^{uv}=s^{uv}=r=l^{R}_{1},\\
(t^{L}_{3})^{uv}=\Big((t^{L}_{1})^{\underline{u}}\cdot\big(\underline{t^{L}_{1}}t^{L}_{3}t^{L}_{1}\big)^{\underline{u}}\cdot(\underline{t^{L}_{1}})^{\underline{u}}\Big)^{u^{2}v}=(t_{1}^{M}t_{3}^{M}\underline{t_{1}^{M}})^{t^{M}_{1}v}=(t_{3}^{M})^{v}=t_{3}^{R},\\
(t^{L}_{4})^{uv}=\big(t_{4}^{M}\big)^{u^{2}v}=\big((t_{4}^{M})^{t^{M}_{1}}\big)^{v}=\big(\underline{t^{R}_{2}}t^{R}_{4}t^{R}_{2}\big)^{l^{R}_{1}t^{R}_{2}\underline{l^{R}_{1}}}\\
(f^{L}_{k})^{uv}=\big((f_{k}^{M})^{t^{M}_{1}}\big)^{v}{\mathrel{\to}^{\operatorname{Co}(f_{k}^{M},t^{M}_{1})}}(f_{k}^{M})^{v}=f_{k}^{R},\end{cases}

as required.
∎

Combining with the fact,
[
QZ1
, Lem. A.2]
, that
any two admissible triangulations in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
are related by a sequence of
\lozenge
-flips.
We know that all
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
are isomorphic for admissible triangulations.
We proceed to show that flip twist groups at admissible triangulations are the whole point groups/fundamental groups of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

5.2.
Simply connectedness

Proposition 5.6
.

For any admissible triangulation
\mathrm{T}_{0}
,
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}),\mathrm{T}_{0})
.

Proof.

We first show that, for any triangulation
\mathrm{T}
, there is a path/morphism

(5.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p\colon\mathrm{T}=\mathrm{T}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{T}_{m}=\mathrm{T}_{0}}

in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, such that
\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{i})\subset\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{i+1})
for any
i
.
To do this, we first keep all the self-folded edges in
\mathrm{T}
(or equivalently cut them),
and (repeatedly) flip
\mathrm{T}
to an admissible triangulation
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
, such that along the way,

\leftmoon(\mathrm{T}_{i})
is not decreasing.
This can be done due to connectedness of flip graphs of triangulations.
Then by
[
QZ1
, Lem. A.2]
again, we can
\lozenge
-flip
\mathrm{T}^{\prime}
to
\mathrm{T}_{0}
to get a path
p
as claimed.
Note that along the way, we can get rid of any loops in
p
since they are redundant.
By formulae in
Lemma

5.2
and
Lemma

5.5
,
the path
p
induces a conjugation
\operatorname{ad}_{p}\colon\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\to\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
, which is an injection.

Now, take any path/morphism
w
from
\mathrm{T}
to
\mathrm{T}_{0}
in
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
We claim that the conjugation
\operatorname{ad}_{w}
above is still an injection.
By
Corollary

5.3
,
\operatorname{ad}_{w}
restricts to an isomorphism
\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
We only need to show that for each loop twist
s\in\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
,
\operatorname{ad}_{w}(s)
is in
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
Use induction on the length of
{w}
starting with the trivial case with
|{w}|=0
.
If there is a loop
r
in the path
w
, i.e.
w=w_{1}rw_{2}
,
then
\operatorname{ad}_{w_{2}}(r)
is in
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
by induction.
As

\operatorname{ad}_{w}(s)=\underline{w_{2}rw_{1}}sw_{1}rw_{2}=\underline{r}^{w_{2}}s^{w_{1}w_{2}}r^{w_{2}}=\underline{\operatorname{ad}_{w_{2}}(r)}\cdot\operatorname{ad}_{w_{1}w_{2}}(s)\cdot\operatorname{ad}_{w_{2}}(r),

we only need to show that
\operatorname{ad}_{w_{1}w_{2}}(s)
is in
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
In other words, we can get rid of all loop twists in
{w}
.
Since
\operatorname{ad}_{w}(s)=(\underline{w}p)\cdot\operatorname{ad}_{p}(s)\cdot(\underline{p}w)
,
we only need to show that
\underline{p}w
is in
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
Note that
\underline{p}w
does not contain any loop twist.
By
Theorem

3.1
, when regarding
\underline{p}w
as a path in
\underline{\operatorname{EG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
it decomposes into (unoriented) squares and pentagons.
Taking account of the orientation, this means that
\underline{p}w
in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
can be written a product of oriented squares/pentagons, together with 2-cycles.
On the other hand, all the 2-cycles are in the 2-cycle twist subgroupoid
by
Corollary

5.3
. Thus,
\underline{p}w
is in
\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})\leq\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
as required.

So we have shown that the conjugations of any 2-cycles and loops
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
by any paths are in
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
On the other hand, let
\underline{\operatorname{\mathcal{EG}}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
be the groupoid obtained from
\underline{\operatorname{EG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
by composing square/pentagon relations.
Consider the canonical functor
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\underline{\operatorname{\mathcal{EG}}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
which sets all 2-cycles and loops equal to 1.
By
Theorem

3.1
,
\pi_{1}\underline{\operatorname{\mathcal{EG}}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
is trivial and hence
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
is generated by 2-cycles and loops.
This implies that
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T}_{0})=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}),\mathrm{T}_{0})
.
∎

An immediate consequence is the following.

Corollary 5.7
.

•

\operatorname{MT}^{\text{\Sun}}=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

•

\overline{\operatorname{FG}_{\text{\tiny{$\copyright$}}}^{\circ}[\mathbb{T}_{0},\mathbb{T}_{0}[1]]}^{\circlearrowleft}=\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
if
\mathbb{T}
is admissible.

Proof.

Choose admissible triangulations
\mathbb{T}
and
\mathrm{T}
,
(
4.6
) implies that
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})=\operatorname{MT}^{\text{\Sun}}
, which implies the first claim.
Combining with (
3.12
), the second claim follows.
∎

Now we can prove the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 5.8
.

\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is a universal cover of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
with covering group
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
and
i_{\mathrm{T}}
in (
4.6
) restricts to a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
,
which further restricts to an isomorphism
\operatorname{CT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{BT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Proof.

In the case when
\mathrm{T}
(and
\mathbb{T}
) is admissible,

i_{\mathrm{T}}
in (
4.6
) becomes
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
,
sending the standard generators to the standard ones.
On the other hand, by
Corollary

2.13
,
there is a presentation of
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
, with three type of relations (e.g.
\operatorname{Co},\operatorname{Br}
and
\operatorname{Br}^{4}
).
Each of which, also holds for (the standard generators of)
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})
, as shown in
Lemma

4.4
.
Thus,
i_{\mathrm{T}}
admits a well-defined inverse that forces it to be an isomorphism.
In particular, in (
4.5
), we have
\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is trivial, i.e.
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is simply connected and is a universal cover of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

In the case when
\mathrm{T}
(and
\mathbb{T}
) is not admissible,
the isomorphism
i_{\mathrm{T}}
restricts to an isomorphism (
4.6
) between subgroups of

\pi_{1}\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}},\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
∎

Another related consequence is that we obtain
Corollary

3.6
therefore.

6.
On fundamental groups of moduli spaces

6.1.
Quadratic differentials

Let
\mathbf{X}
be a compact Riemann surface and
\omega_{\mathbf{X}}
be its holomorphic cotangent bundle.
A
meromorphic quadratic differential

\phi
on
\mathbf{X}
is a meromorphic section
of the line bundle
\omega_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}
.
In terms of a local coordinate at
z
on
\mathbf{X}
,

\phi
can be written as
\phi(z)=g(z)\,dz^{2}
, for a meromorphic function

g(z)=z^{d}(a_{d}+\cdots)

for
a_{d}\neq 0
.
If
d>0
,
z
is a
zero
of
order

d
; if
d<0
,
z
is a
pole
of
order

-d
;
if
d=0
,
z
is a
smooth
point.
The zeros and poles are singularities/critical points of
\phi
.
For
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
,
denote by
\mathbf{w}_{d}(\phi)
the set of zeroes of order
d
and
\mathbf{w}_{-d}(\phi)
the set of poles of order
d
.
There are two type of singularities:

•

the finite type, including all zeroes and simple (i.e. order 1) poles.
Let

\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}=(\mathbf{w}_{-1},\mathbf{w}_{0},\mathbf{w}_{1},\cdots)

be the collection of them.

•

the infinite type, including all poles with order at least two.
Let

\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}=(\mathbf{w}_{-2},\mathbf{w}_{-3},\cdots)

be the collection of them.

Denote by
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}=\mathbf{X}^{\circ}(\phi)=\mathbf{X}\smallsetminus(\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}\cup\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})
.

In general, one could also allow a set
\mathbf{w}_{0}(\phi)
of special smooth markings of
\phi
in
\mathbf{X}
.

Foliation structure

The key structure of quadratic differentials is the
foliation
structure.
At a smooth point of
\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
,
there is a distinguished local coordinate
\omega=\int\sqrt{g(z)}dz
,
uniquely defined up to transformations:
\omega\mapsto\pm\,\omega+\operatorname{const}
,
with respect to which, one has
\phi(\omega)=d\omega\otimes d\omega
.
Then by locally pulling back the Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{C}
using a distinguished coordinate
\omega
,

\phi
induces the
\phi
-metric and geodesics on
\mathbf{X}
.
Each geodesics have a constant phase with respect to
\omega
.

A
trajectory
of a
\phi
on
\mathbf{X}
is a maximal geodesic
\gamma\colon(0,1)\to\mathbf{X}^{\circ}
,
with respect to the
\phi
metric (i.e. corresponds to the equation
\operatorname{Im}\omega\equiv\operatorname{const}
).
When
\lim\gamma(t)
exists in
\mathbf{X}
as
t\to 0
(resp.
t\to 1
),
the limits are called the left (resp. right) endpoint of
\gamma
.
The
horizontal
trajectories (with
\operatorname{Im}\omega\equiv 0
) of a meromeorphic quadratic differential
\phi

provide the
horizontal foliation
on
\mathbf{X}
.

Figure 18.
Foliation structure around a point of a Riemann surface

In
Figure

18
, one has examples of (horizontal) foliations. Namely:

•

On the left column, the pictures show the foliation at a simple zero/smooth point/simple pole (up to bottom). In general, there are
d+2
distinguished directions at a finite singularity (conical point) of order
d\geq-1

that divides the neighbourhood of such a point into
d+2
(local) half planes at zero.

•

In the middle column, the pictures show the foliation at a double pole
p
depending on the residue

(6.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{p}(\phi)\colon=\pm 2\int_{\widetilde{\delta_{p}}}\sqrt{\phi},

of
\phi
at
p
, where the integration is along the lift
\widetilde{\delta_{p}}
of a small loop
\delta_{p}

on the spectral cover (w.r.t.
\phi
) of
\mathbf{X}
.

•

On the right column, the pictures show the foliation at a pole of order 3/4/5 (up to bottom).
In general, there are
|d|-2
distinguished directions at a pole of order
d\leq-3

that divides the neighbourhood of such a point into
d+2
(local) half planes at infinity.

Horizonal strip decomposition

There are the following types of trajectories of a quadratic differential:

•

saddle trajectories
, either whose both ends are in
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}
.

•

separating trajectories
with one endpoint in
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}
and the other in
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}
;

•

generic trajectories
whose both ends are in
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}
;

•

recurrent
if at least one of its directions is recurrent.

But from now on, we now fix the horizonal direction to unless specified otherwise,
i.e. trajectories means horizontal trajectories.
A
saddle connection
is a trajectory in some arbitrary direction
and a saddle trajectory means a horizontal saddle connection.

Removing from
\mathbf{X}
the separating trajectories and saddle trajectories,
it decomposes the surface into connected components,
each of which is of the following types:

•

a
horizontal strip
, i.e. is isomorphic to

\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid a<\operatorname{Im}(z)<b\}

equipped with the differential
dz^{2}
for some
a<b\in\mathbb{R}
.
It is swept out by generic trajectories connecting two (not necessarily distinct) poles.

•

a
half-plane
, i.e. is isomorphic to

\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\operatorname{Im}(z)>0\}

equipped with the differential
dz^{2}
.
It is swept out by generic trajectories which connect a fixed pole to itself.

•

a
ring domain
or
cylinder
, i.e. is foliated by closed trajectories.

•

a
spiral domain
, i.e. is the interior of the closure of a recurrent trajectory.

We call this union the
horizontal strip decomposition
of
\mathbf{X}
with respect to
\phi
.

A quadratic differential
\phi
on
\mathbf{X}
is
saddle-free
, if it has no saddle trajectory.
Then there are only half-planes and horizontal strips by
[
BS
, Lem. 3.1]
.
Note that in such a case:

•

In each horizontal strip, the trajectories are isotopic to each other.

•

The boundary of any component consists of separating trajectories.

•

In each horizontal strip, there is a unique geodesic,
the
saddle connection
, connecting the two zeroes on its boundary.

Figure 19.
A loop in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
with a missing center in the partial compactification

Example 6.1
.

Figure
19
shows several local horizontal strip decompositions on
\mathbf{X}
corresponding to
the motion of a L-flip at a self-folded triangle (the top picture).
In particular, there is a ‘wall crossing’ state (the bottom picture)
when there is a saddle trajectory (red cycle),
which bounds a degenerate ring domain (meaning one of its boundary components is a double pole).
Note that, in the pictures

•

the emerald/blue vertices (bullets) are poles or marked points,

•

the black/red suns are double/simple poles respectively,

•

the red vertices (small circles) are simple zeroes,

•

the emerald arcs are (generic) geodesics,

•

the blue arcs are separating trajectories,

•

the red solid arc/cycle is a saddle trajectory (in the bottom picture)
and the red dashed arc is a saddle connection (in the top picture).

Moreover,

Figure

19
demonstrates a loop in the moduli space of quadratic differentials
which corresponds to the L-twist (as the bottom picture of
Figure

2
) of the saddle connection in the top picture.

For comparison, see the left picture of
Figure

1
(or
[
KQ2
, Fig. 4.3]
)
for the horizontal strip decompositions pictures of another loop in the moduli space,
which corresponds to a braid twist (as the top picture of
Figure

2
).

The
real blow-up
of
\mathbf{X}
with respect to
\phi
is a weighted DMSp
\mathbf{X}^{\phi}_{\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}}
obtained from
\mathbf{X}
by replacing each puncture
p
in
\mathbf{w}_{\leq-3}
with a boundary component
\partial_{p}
with
\operatorname{ord}_{\phi}(p)-2
marked points
and regarding
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}=\Delta
as decoration set with weights given by the degree of the finite singularities,
\mathbf{w}_{-2}=\text{\Sun}
as puncture set.
One can also forget about the decorations and denote by
\mathbf{X}^{\phi}
the corresponding weighted marked surface with punctures.

Moduli spaces and framing

The
moduli space

\operatorname{Quad}(g)
of quadratic differentials on genus
g
Riemann surfaces decomposes into many strata,
parameterized by their prescribed singularity type, i.e.

\operatorname{Quad}(g)=\bigsqcup_{|\mathbf{w}|=4g-4}\operatorname{Quad}(g;\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}),

where

(6.2)

\|\mathbf{w}\|=\|\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}\|+\|\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}\|\colon=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}k|\mathbf{w}_{k}|

satisfying
\|\mathbf{w}\|=4g-4
,
for
|\mathbf{w}_{k}|
the number of points in
\mathbf{w}_{k}
.

The
numerical data
of singularity type of
\phi
on
\mathbf{X}
is
(\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})
,
which can be encoded by the associated weighted DMSp
\mathbf{X}^{\phi}_{\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}}
.
We will only consider the strum
\operatorname{Quad}(g;\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})
for
\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}=\mathbf{w}_{1}
(i.e. only simple zeros as finite singularities) in this section and

\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}}\neq\mathbf{w}_{-2}
(i.e. not only just double poles as infinite singularities).
In such a case, a quadratic differential in
\operatorname{Quad}(g;\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})
is parameterized by a DMSp
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}

(with trivial weight).

More precisely, a point
(\mathbf{X},\phi,\psi)
in

\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{Quad}(g;\mathbf{w}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{inf}})

consists of a Riemann surface
\mathbf{X}
with quadratic differential
\phi
,
equipped with a diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}
, preserving the marked points and punctures.
Two such quadratic differentials
(\mathbf{X}_{1},\phi_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(\mathbf{X}_{2},\phi_{2},\psi_{2})

are equivalent, if there exists a biholomorphism
f\colon\mathbf{X}_{1}\to\mathbf{X}_{2}

such that
f^{*}(\phi_{2})=\phi_{1}
and furthermore
\psi_{2}^{-1}\circ f_{*}\circ\psi_{1}\in\operatorname{Diff}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
where
f_{*}\colon\mathbf{X}_{1}^{\phi_{1}}\to\mathbf{X}_{2}^{\phi_{2}}
is the induced diffeomorphism.

As discussed in
[
KQ2
, § 4.1]
, there are two natural way to frame on
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
Namely, in the definition above, if we change
\operatorname{Diff}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
to
\operatorname{Diff}_{0}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
to identify equivalent elements,
then we obtain the
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed moduli space
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
Similarly, we can also use
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
to frame, i.e. replacing the diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}

by a diffeomorphism
\psi\colon\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}_{\mathbf{w}_{1}}
preserving the marked points, punctures and decorations.
These three moduli spaces fits in the following diagram:

(6.3)

\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

For a
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed
the generic trajectories on
\mathbf{X}
(with respect to
\phi
)
are inherited by
\mathbf{S}
, for any
\psi\colon\mathbf{S}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}
, and
all trajectories on
\mathbf{X}
(with respect to
\phi
)
are inherited by
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
, for any
\Psi\colon\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}
.

So generic trajectories become open arcs on
\mathbf{S}
(as well as on
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
)
and saddle trajectories/connections becomes closed arcs on
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}
.

Example 6.2
.

The foliations in
Figure

19
are precisely a loop in the once-punctured monogon case,
i.e.
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{Quad}(0;1,-2,-3)
.
One can write down the quadratic differentials globally (on
\mathbb{C}\mathbb{P}^{1}
):

\operatorname{Quad}(0;1,-2,-3)=\{c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\mid\phi(z)=\frac{z+c}{z^{2}}dz^{2}\}.

More precisely

•

The residue at the double pole (i.e. zero) is
s=\operatorname{Res}_{0}(\phi)=4\pi\mathbf{i}\sqrt{c}
.

•

The central foliation in
Figure

19
correspond to the
c=0
case:
then the quadratic differential
\phi(z)=z^{-1}dz^{2}
is in
\operatorname{Quad}(0;-1,-3)
.

Moreover, in this case, we have

•

\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
.

•

\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\{\operatorname{log}(c)\mid c\in\mathbb{C}^{*}\}\cong\mathbb{C}
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-cover/universal cover of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

6.2.
Cell structures

Stratification

Let
\Psi\in\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
One has the following numerical equality:

r_{\Psi}+2s_{\Psi}+t_{\Psi}=\|\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}\|+2|\mathbf{w}_{\operatorname{fin}}|=\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}(\mathbf{w}_{k}+2)=\colon K

where
s_{\Psi}/r_{\Psi}/t_{\Psi}
are the numbers of saddle/recurrent/separating trajectories, respectively.

There is a natural filtration of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
given by

B_{0}=B_{1}\subset B_{2}\subset\cdots\subset B_{K}=\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}),

for

(6.4)

\displaystyle B_{p}=B_{p}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\{\Psi\in\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}):r_{\Psi}+2s_{\Psi}\leq p\},

observing that
B_{0}=B_{1}
is the set of saddle-free differentials.
The corresponding
stratification
is given by
F_{p}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=B_{p}\smallsetminus B_{p-1}
.

The space
B_{2}
is called the space of
tame differentials
,
We observe that
F_{0}
is dense,
F_{1}
is empty, and
F_{2}
consists of differentials with exactly one saddle trajectory.
Here are two more precisely statements, which was proved in
[
BS
]
and are generalized in
[
BMQS
]
.

Lemma 6.3
.

[
BMQS
, Lem. 4.1]

\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\mathbb{C}\cdot B_{0}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
and hence
B_{0}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is dense in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
Moreover,
F_{2}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
has codimension 1 and
B_{2}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
has codimension 2.

Lemma 6.4
.

[
BMQS
, Cor. 4.3]

The connected components of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
naturally correspond to the connected components of
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

All the statements above also apply to the
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed version.

WKB-triangulations

Let

\mathbf{H}_{\circ}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\mid\operatorname{Im}(z)>0\}\subset\mathbb{C}

be the strict upper half plane and
\mathbf{H}=\mathbf{H}_{\circ}\cup\mathbb{R}_{<0}
the half-open-half-closed one.

Let
\Psi
be a saddle-free
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed quadratic differential.
Then there is an associated
(WKB-)triangulation

\mathbb{T}_{\Psi}
of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
,
where the arcs are (isotopy classes of inherited) generic trajectories.
The dual graph
\mathbb{T}_{\Psi}^{*}
consisting of saddle connections.
Denote by
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})
be the subspace in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
consisting of
those saddle-free
\Psi
support on
\mathrm{T}
(i.e. whose associated triangulation is
\mathrm{T}
).
Then
\operatorname{U}(\mathrm{T})\cong\mathbf{H}^{\mathrm{T}}
and

(6.5)

\displaystyle B_{0}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\bigcup_{\mathrm{T}\in\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T}).

for
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})\cong\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}}
.
The coordinates
(u_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\mathrm{T}}
give the complex modulus of the horizontal strip with generic trajectory in the isotopy class
\gamma
.
Thus the
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})
are precisely the connected components of
B_{0}(\mathbf{S})
.
The boundary of
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})
meets
F_{2}(\mathbf{S})
in
2n
connected components,
which we denote
\partial^{\sharp}_{\gamma}\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})
and
\partial^{\flat}_{\gamma}\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T})
,
where the coordinate
u_{\gamma}
goes to the negative or positive real axis, respectively.
Note that
u_{\gamma}
cannot go to zero since it means the collision of two zeros.

By Walls-have-Ends property (
[
BS
, Prop 5.8]
for simple zero case and
[
BMQS
, Cor. 4.3]
in general),
we have the following, showing that
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is a skeleton for
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Lemma 6.5
.

There is a (unique up to homotopy) canonical embedding

(6.6)

\wp_{\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}}\colon\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})

whose image is dual to
B_{2}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})

and which induces a surjective map

(6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wp_{*}\colon\pi_{1}\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}).}

Again, these statements also apply to the
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed version.
For the later use, we record them as well:
there is a canonical embedding

(6.8)

\wp_{\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}}\colon\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\to\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

that induces a surjective map

(6.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wp_{*}\colon\pi_{1}\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}).}

Parallel to the cell structure of moduli spaces of quadratic differentials,
one has stability structures on triangulated categories, see
Section

A.2
.
We recall a upgraded version of
Theorem

3.7
.

Theorem 6.6
.

[
CHQ
, Thm. 1.1]

The isomorphism (
3.4
) can be complexified to an isomorphism between complex manifolds

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rcl}\operatorname{X}^{\text{\Sun}}_{*}\colon\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})&\cong&\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})),\\
s\cdot\Psi&\mapsto&s\cdot(Z,\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathbb{T}_{\Psi}}),\end{array}

for any
s\in\mathbb{C}
and saddle-free
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed quadratic differential
\Phi
, where

(6.12)

Z(X_{\eta})=\int_{\widetilde{\eta}}\sqrt{\Psi}.

In
Appendix

B
, we will study a quotient version of (
6.6
):

(6.13)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))\operatorname{Aut}^{\circ},

where
\operatorname{Aut}^{\circ}
is a quotient group of the autoequivalence group of
\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
, cf. (
B.8
).

6.3.
The kernel of AJ map as the fundamental group

Proposition 6.7
.

Under the embedding (
6.6
) and the isomorphism (
6.6
),
any square, pentagon, S-DB, L-DB or Sym-Hex in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
is contractible in
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))
.

Proof.

The square, pentagon and the S-DB follows precisely as in the proof of
[
KQ2
, Prop.4.14]
.
The other two new relations (L-DB and Sym-Hex) follows in a similar fashion.
We sketch the proof as follows.

By
Theorem

3.7
,
a L-DB or Sym-Hex in
\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
induces a corresponding hexagon in
\operatorname{EG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))

as shown in
Figure

20
or
Figure

21
respectively.

Figure 20.
L-DB in
\operatorname{EG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))

Figure 21.
Sym-Hex in
\operatorname{EG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))

In fact, such an induced hexagon can be described in the same way in
\operatorname{EG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))

by setting the simples
X_{1},X_{2}
in the source heart
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0}
correspond to the proper (graded) closed arcs
as the formulae shown in the left of the figures.
Then there are always two short exact sequences in
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0}

(6.14)

\displaystyle 0\to X_{2}\to X_{+}\to X_{1}\to 0,

(6.15)

\displaystyle 0\to X_{1}\to X_{-}\to X_{+}\to 0.

and the same hexagon of hearts (=
\operatorname{HH}_{0}
) in
\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))
with

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{i}=(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0})_{X_{i}}^{\sharp},\;i=1,2\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{21}=(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{2})_{X_{+}}^{\sharp},\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{12}=(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{1})_{X_{-}}^{\sharp},\\
\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{3}=(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{21})_{X_{1}}^{\sharp}=(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{12})_{X_{2}}^{\sharp},\end{cases}

which consists of two paths:

\begin{cases}\operatorname{HH}_{0}^{+}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{21}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{3},\\
\operatorname{HH}_{0}^{-}\colon\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{12}\to\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{3}.\end{cases}

Note that the rigidities of the simple tiltings are different in these two cases.

Now we use the
\mathbb{C}
-action to show that such induced hexagons are contractible.
Namely, we can choose
\sigma_{\pm}
in
\operatorname{U}(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0})
such that

(6.16)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}Z_{+}(X_{1}[1])=\exp 3\pi\mathbf{i}\delta,&\\
Z_{+}(X_{2}[1])=\exp\pi\mathbf{i}\delta,&\end{cases}\quad\begin{cases}Z_{-}(X_{1}[1])=\exp\pi\mathbf{i}\delta,&\\
Z_{-}(X_{2}[1])=\exp 3\pi\mathbf{i}\delta,&\end{cases}

(6.17)

\displaystyle Z_{\pm}(X)=M\cdot\exp\textstyle{\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi}{2}},\qquad X\in\operatorname{Sim}\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\smallsetminus\{X_{1},X_{2}\}

for some real number
M\gg 1
.
for some small
\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and large
M\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
as shown in
Figure

22
.

\cdot\epsilon
x
y
\bullet
Z_{+}
Z_{2}
Z_{1}
Z(X)
\cdot\epsilon
x
y
\bullet
Z_{-}
Z_{1}
Z_{2}
Z(X)

Figure 22.
Central charges of
\sigma_{+},\sigma_{-}

Then we can deduce from (
6.14
) that

X_{\pm}
is stale with respect to
\sigma_{\pm}
but unstable with respect to
\sigma_{\mp}
.
Furthermore, by the simple tilting formulae in
[
CHQ
, App. A]
,
we deduce that
k\delta\cdot\sigma_{+}
, for
k=1,2,3
, are in the three tilting walls of the path

\operatorname{HH}_{0}^{+}
while
(k\delta-\delta,k\delta)\cdot\sigma_{+}
is in the corresponding cube
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{l})

for
k/l=1/0,2/2,3/21,4/3
.
Hence we deduce that
[0,4\delta]\cdot\sigma_{+}
is homotopy to
\wp_{*}(\operatorname{HH}_{0}^{+})
.
Similarly, we have
[0,4\delta]\cdot\sigma_{-}
is homotopy to
\wp_{*}(\operatorname{HH}_{0}^{-})
.
Then if we connect
\sigma_{\pm}
with a line segment
l
in
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{0})
,
we see that
4\delta\cdot l
will be in
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\operatorname{\mathcal{H}}_{3})
and
the boundary of the rectangle
[0,4\delta]\cdot l
is homotopy to
\wp_{*}(\operatorname{HH}_{0})
.
Thus,
\wp_{*}(\operatorname{HH}_{0})
is contractible as required.
∎

By
Equation

6.6
and
Proposition

6.7
, the simply connectedness of
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
in
Theorem

5.8

implies the simply connectedness of
\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))
.

Theorem 6.8
.

\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathbb{T}}(\operatorname{\mathcal{D}}_{3}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}))
is simply connected and hence is a universal cover of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Combining
Theorem

2.6
(that
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}
),
Theorem

5.8
(that
\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})
)
and the characterization of connected components (in
Corollary

3.6
and
Lemma

6.4
),
we obtain our main theorem.

Theorem 6.9
.

Let
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
be a DMSp. Then we have

(6.18)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\ker\operatorname{AJ}

and

(6.19)

\begin{cases}\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=1,\\
\pi_{0}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}).\end{cases}

6.4.
Corollaries

Extending the Abel-Jacobi map to
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})

The following is a version of Octahedron Axiom in the group setting.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
P\lhd G
be a normal subgroup of
G
and
f\colon G\to G_{0}
a group homomorphism such that

f\mid_{P}
is surjective. Denote by
S=\ker(f)
and
M=\ker(f\circ i)
, where
i
is the inclusion
P\to G
.
Then we have

•

M=P\cap S
and
G=P\cdot S
.

•

G/P\cong S/M
and
G/M=(G/P)\times(P/M)
.

So there is the following octahedron:

(6.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S/M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G/P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Proof.

It is straightforward see
M=P\cap S
. Then by the Diamond (=
2^{nd}
Isomorphism) Theorem,
we have
PS/S\cong P/M=G_{0}=G/S
. Thus,
G=PS
.
Using the Diamond Theorem again, we have
G/P\cong S/M
.
Finally, quotienting by
M
, we have
G/M=(P/M)\cdot(S/M)
.
But
(P/M)\cap(S/M)=(P\cap S)/M=1
, which implies that
G/M=(P/M)\times(S/M)
.
∎

Taking
S=\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\lhd\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=G
,
G_{0}=\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
and
f=F^{\Delta}_{*}
.
Then
M=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
,
P=\pi_{1}\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
and (
6.20
) becomes (
6.21
) in the introduction.

Corollary 6.11
.

We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\pi_{1}\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\cong\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\times\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S})

that fit into the octahedron (
6.21
).

(6.21)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{AJ}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\Delta}_{*}}

7.
On fundamental groups of partially compactified spaces

7.1.
Partial compactification

Consider a
\{1,2\}
-valued function
\mathbf{k}\colon\text{\Sun}\to\{1,2\}
.
We write
\mathbf{k}_{i}=\mathbf{k}^{-1}(i)
,
i=1,2
, for simplicity.

Definition 7.1
.

Denote by
\operatorname{FQuad}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
the moduli space of

\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
-framed quadratic differentials with parameter
\mathbf{k}
,
where a point
(\mathbf{X},\phi,\psi)
in it consists of a quadratic differential
\phi
on
\mathbf{X}

with a diffeomorphism/framing
\psi\colon\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}\to\mathbf{X}^{\phi}_{\mathbf{w}_{1}(\phi)}
, sending

•

the punctures in
\mathbf{k}_{2}\subset\text{\Sun}
to the double poles in
\mathbf{w}_{2}(\phi)
and

•

the ‘collided points’
\mathbf{k}_{1}\subset\text{\Sun}
to simple poles in
\mathbf{w}_{1}(\phi)
.

In particular, the number
|\mathbf{w}_{1}(\phi)|
of simple zeros of
\phi
equals
|\Delta|-|\mathbf{k}_{1}|
,
determined by the equation
\|\mathbf{w}\|=4g-4
.
Two such data
(\mathbf{X}_{i},\phi_{i},\psi_{i})
are equivalent if there exists a biholomorphism
f\colon\mathbf{X}_{1}\to\mathbf{X}_{2}

such that
f^{*}(\phi_{2})=\phi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}^{-1}\circ f_{*}\circ\psi_{1}\in\operatorname{Diff}_{0}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

Now we fix a partition
\text{\Sun}=\leftmoon\cup{\rightmoon}
and make the punctures in
{\rightmoon}
collidable.
Define the
moduli space of quadratic differentials on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}}
to be the union

(7.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})=\coprod_{\mathbf{k}(\leftmoon)=2}\operatorname{FQuad}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}),

which is a partial compactification of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
As in
[
BS
, § 6.5]
, the period coordinate of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
extends to
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.

Remark 7.2
.

The mapping class group
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})\lhd\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
naturally acts on
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

(by left composing with the framing).
However,
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
does not act on it since one can not permute a usual puncture with a collidable one,
except for the cases
(\leftmoon,{\rightmoon})=(\text{\Sun},\varnothing)
and
(\leftmoon,{\rightmoon})=(\varnothing,\text{\Sun})
,
where
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})=\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.

\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\subset\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
is called the
complete
part,
consisting of the differentials without simple poles.
The incomplete part is denoted by

\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})\colon=\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})\smallsetminus\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}).

7.2.
Killing loops

Let
\mu({\rightmoon})
be the set of (forward) L-flips in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
with the corresponding isolated punctures in
{\rightmoon}
.
We will regard L-flips in
\mu({\rightmoon})
as loops/elements in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

under the map (
6.9
).
By
Figure

19
, we see that any L-flip in
\mu({\rightmoon})
becomes contractible in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
after the partial compactification.
We claim that this is essentially the only type of loops
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
that get killed in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\operatorname{c}
be a loop in
\operatorname{FG}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
. If it is contractible in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
,
then, it decomposes to a finite sum of L-flips in
\mu({\rightmoon})

in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
up to homotopy.

Proof.

\operatorname{FQuad}_{\mathbf{k}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
has complex co-dimension
|\mathbf{k}_{1}|
in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.
Let
R(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
be the subspace of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
, consisting of saddle-free quadratic differentials
with exactly one simple pole.
These are the ‘top strata’ in the incomplete part
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

with real co-dimension two in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.
The rest of
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
has real co-dimension at least three in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.

Let
D
be a disk with
\partial D=\operatorname{c}
that is contractible in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.
By perturbation, we can make
D
only intersect
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
in
R(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

and intersect
R(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
transversally.
By dimension reason, the intersection consists of isolated points.
If
D
does not intersect
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
, then it is trivial and there is nothing to prove.
Otherwise, we can decompose
D
into many small disks such that each of which
only intersect
R(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
at one point.
We only need to show that the assertion holds for each disk.
In other words, we may assume that
D
intersects
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
at one point
\Psi_{0}

and we only need to show that it is homotopy to an L-flip in
\mu({\rightmoon})
.

As
\Psi_{0}
has exactly one simple pole and is saddle-free,
it admits a WKB-triangulation with exactly one triangle containing a simple pole with foliation shown in the center picture of
Figure

19
, and all other triangles are once-decorated (i.e. with a simple zero).
Then there is a saddle-free differential
\Psi
in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
, cf. the top picture of
Figure

19
, such that
\Psi_{0}
is the closure (taken in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
) of the cell

\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T}_{\Psi})\subset B_{0}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\subset\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}),

where
\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}
is the WKB-triangulation associated to
\Psi
.
Note that
\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}
is the same WKB-triangulation for
\Psi_{0}
(after forgetting about the simple zeros and simple poles) and let
T_{0}
be the self-folded triangle in
\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}
containing the simple pole of
\Psi_{0}
.
Recall from
Section

6.2
that
\operatorname{U}^{\circ}(\mathrm{T}_{\Psi})\cong\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}
with coordinate
(u_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}
.
Let
u_{0}
be the one corresponding to the saddle connection in
T_{0}

(dual to self-folded edge
\gamma_{0}
, cf. dashed red arc in the top picture of
Figure

19
)
and then
\Psi_{0}
is in the boundary

\partial_{0}=\{(u_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}\mid u_{0}=0\}\subset\partial\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}.

In fact, a neighbourhood of
\Psi_{0}
in
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
lies in
\partial_{0}
.
Therefore,
we deduce that when taking a neighbourhood
N_{D}(\Psi_{0})
of
\Psi_{0}
in
D
, we have

\left(N_{D}(\Psi_{0})\smallsetminus\{\Psi_{0}\}\right)\subset\left(\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}\bigsqcup\partial^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{0}}\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}\right),

where
\partial^{\sharp}_{\gamma_{0}}\mathbf{H}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}}
is the wall containing differential with exactly one saddle trajectory in the triangle
T_{0}
, cf. the bottom picture in
Figure

19
.
Hence we see that
\partial N_{D}(\Psi_{0})
is homotopy to (up to sign) the L-flip

\mu_{\gamma_{0}}^{\sharp}\colon\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}\to\mathrm{T}_{\Psi}.

By the assumption that
D
is contractible in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
,
we see that

\operatorname{c}=\partial D\backsimeq\partial N_{D}(\Psi_{0})\backsimeq\pm\mu_{\gamma_{0}}^{\sharp}

in
\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
as required.
∎

Recall that
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\rightmoon})
consists of L-arcs of
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
enclosing exactly a puncture in
{\rightmoon}

and
\operatorname{L}({\rightmoon})
is the normal subgroup in
\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
generated by all L-twists along L-arcs in
\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\rightmoon})
.
We have the following corollary of the calculation of fundamental groups from
Theorem

6.9
.

Theorem 7.4
.

The fundamental group of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
is

(7.2)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\mu({\rightmoon})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{L}({\rightmoon})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon}),

where
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon}
is the DMSp obtained from
\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}}
by deleting punctures in
{\rightmoon}
.

Note that
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon}
does not satisfy (
2.18
).

Proof.

Denote by
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
the groupoid obtained from
\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
by adding the relations

\mu=1
for any L-flip
\mu
in
\mu({\rightmoon})
.

Choose a saddle-free different
\Psi
in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
with admissible WKB-triangulation
\mathrm{T}
.
By (
6.18
), we have that

\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}},\Psi)\cong\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}},\mathrm{T})\;(=\operatorname{FT}(\mathrm{T})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbb{T})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})),

where
\mathbb{T}
is a decorated triangulation satisfying
\mathrm{T}=F_{*}^{\Delta}(\mathbb{T})
.
By
Proposition

7.3
, we know that

\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}},\Psi)=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\mu({\rightmoon})\cong\pi_{1}\operatorname{\mathcal{FG}}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}},\mathrm{T})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{L}({\rightmoon}),

so that we have (
7.2
) except for the last equation,
which then follows from the presentation of
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
in
Theorem

2.12
,
comparing to the one of
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon})
.
∎

7.3.
Orbifolding

There are two reasons for orbifolding.
On the one hand, the resulting moduli spaces match with the categorical construction in
Appendix

B
.
On the other hand, the extra strata play the role of hyperplanes, which should get
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbifolding in the quotient spaces, cf.
Appendix

C
for examples.

Signed quadratic differentials

A quadratic differential on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
is a
signed quadratic differential

(\Psi,\epsilon)
,
consisting of
\Psi=[\mathbf{X},\phi,\psi]\in\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
and
together with a choice
\epsilon(V)
of signs of the residue
\operatorname{Res}_{V}(\phi)
at each
V\in\mathbf{k}_{2}\cap{\rightmoon}
,
cf. (
6.1
), and a random choice of signs at each
V\in\mathbf{k}_{1}
.
Two such differentials
(\Psi_{i},\epsilon_{i})
are equivalent if
\Psi_{1}=\Psi_{2}
in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

and
\epsilon_{1}(V)=\epsilon_{2}(V)
for all
V\in\mathbf{k}_{2}\cap{\rightmoon}
.

Denote by
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
the moduli space of quadratic differentials on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
.
Then the
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
-action on
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
upgrades to a
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
-action on
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
,
which is free only when restricted to the complete part,
where
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
is defined in (
B.5
).

Definition 7.5
.

The
moduli space of signed quadratic differentials on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
is defined to be the orbifold

(7.3)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\colon=\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})/\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}).

Example 7.6
.

Following
Example

6.2
, we have

\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\varnothing,\text{\YinYang}})=\big(\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\operatorname{FQuad}(0;1,-2,-3)\big)\bigsqcup\operatorname{FQuad}(0;-1,-3).

The complete part
\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\operatorname{FQuad}(0;1,-2,-3)
is a double cover of
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
For instance, the purple loop in
Figure

19
lifts to the purple loop in
Figure

18
.

Instead of calculating the fundamental group of
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
,
let us look at a
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
-covering orbifold of which:

(7.4)

\displaystyle\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\colon=\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})/\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}.

So we have the following commutative diagram

(7.5)

\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
\operatorname{Quad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

Similar to a comment in
[
BS
, § 6.3]
, that
the only difference between the spaces
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
and
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
is some extra orbifolding along the incomplete locus
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
.

Orbifold fundamental groups

Let
\mu^{2}({\rightmoon})
be the set of squares of L-flips in
\mu({\rightmoon})
and

(7.6)

\displaystyle\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})\colon=\langle L_{\zeta}^{2}\mid\zeta\in\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\rightmoon})\rangle\quad(\lhd\operatorname{L}({\rightmoon})\lhd\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})).

Theorem 7.7
.

The fundamental group of
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
is

(7.7)

\displaystyle\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})=\pi_{1}\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})/\mu^{2}({\rightmoon})\cong\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})/\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon}).

Proof.

The top strata
R(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
in the incomplete locus
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
has precisely extra
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-orbifolding.
Other part of
\operatorname{IC}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
will have higher
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{\oplus k}
-orbifolding, for some
k\geq 2
,
but they don’t effect the fundamental group due to co-dimension reason.

By the proof of
Proposition

7.3
, we know that
\mu^{2}({\rightmoon})
are all the loops in
\operatorname{FQuad}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})

get killed after partial compactification and orbifolding. Thus the theorem follows as
Theorem

7.4
.
∎

7.4.
Decorated Marked Surfaces with mixed punctures & vortices

Symmetry groups for DMSx

Denote by
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}}
the DMSp with a fixed partition
\text{\Sun}=\leftmoon\cup{\rightmoon}
of punctures,
which differs from
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
when considering mapping class groups,
similar to
Remark

7.2
.
Namely,
\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})
requires to preserve
\leftmoon
and
{\rightmoon}
setwise separately and

\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
only requires to preserve
☉
setwise. Hence, we have

\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})\lhd\operatorname{MCG}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.
Note that such a change does not effect surface braid group and mixed twist groups, e.g.

\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})=\operatorname{SBr}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}).

Next, we turn the punctures in
{\rightmoon}
into vortices.
Let
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
be the DMS with mixed punctures & vortices (DMSx).
The topological difference (when considering symmetry groups) between
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
and
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}}
is
the following extra relations for
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
:

•

L_{\zeta}^{2}=1
for any
\zeta\in\operatorname{LA}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\rightmoon})
.

For
\operatorname{Sym}^{*}
being
\operatorname{MCG},\operatorname{SBr}
or
\operatorname{MT}
,
\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})
is a normal subgroup of
\operatorname{Sym}^{*}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})

and thus we define

\operatorname{Sym}^{*}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})\colon=\operatorname{Sym}^{*}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}})/\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon}).

In the case when
\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})
is not a normal subgroup of some symmetry groups associated to
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,{\rightmoon}}
,
we need to quotient the intersection of which
or quotient a quotient group of which.
More precisely:

•

Let
\operatorname{CO}({\rightmoon})=\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})\cap\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})
and define

(7.8)

\displaystyle\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})\colon=\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}},{\leftmoon})/\operatorname{CO}({\rightmoon}).

•

Let
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}^{2}({\rightmoon})=\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})/\operatorname{CO}({\rightmoon})
be the abelian subgroup of
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})
,
generated by the square of the standard generators of
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})
.
Define

\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\text{\YinYang})=\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}({\rightmoon})/\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}^{2}({\rightmoon})\cong\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{{\oplus\text{\YinYang}}}.

We will write
\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\YinYang}})\cong\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\mathbf{S})\oplus\operatorname{\bf H}_{1}(\text{\YinYang})
.

Now, we can quotient the whole octahedron in
Figure

6
by
\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})
and obtain a short exact sequence of octahedrons in
Figure

23
.
The groups in the quotient octahedron are the ones for DMSx
\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
,
which we will use to analysis the fundamental groups of quadratic differentials on
\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}}
.

,

(among other things).

Flip graphs for DMSx

Recall that we have constructed the flip graph
\operatorname{FG}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
in

as skeleton of
\operatorname{FQuad}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})

B.10

Now, we give an alternative construction of
\operatorname{FG}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\leftmoon,\text{\YinYang}})
and a
\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})

via the flip graph
\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
.

We first take various quotient graphs of
\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})
by various groups in

(in red),
as shown in the left half of

.
In particular, we have

\operatorname{FG}^{\pm}(\mathbf{S}^{\text{\Sun}})\colon=\frac{\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}/{\rightmoon}^{2}})}{\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})}=\frac{{\operatorname{FG}^{\mathbb{T}}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\text{\Sun}})}/{\operatorname{L}^{2}({\rightmoon})}}{\operatorname{MT}(\mathbf{S}_{\Delta}^{\leftmoon/{\rightmoon}^{2}})}.

Note that these group actions are all free and hence they preserve all the relations
(i.e. square/pentagon/hexagon/dumbell).
Thus, we have the corresponding flip groupoids (which will be used in the next subsection).
